**Explanation:** One of the key benefits of studying history, as mentioned in the text, is the development of critical thinking skills through the process of analyzing evidence, identifying bias, and constructing arguments.

**3. The branch of history that focuses on state formation, governments, and the exercise of power is known as...**
A. Social History.
B. Economic History.
C. Cultural History.
D. Political History.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** Political History is defined as the branch that focuses on political events, leaders, governments, state formation, and the use of power.

**4. A historian studying family life, gender roles, and the experiences of ordinary people is primarily engaged in...**
A. Diplomatic History.
B. Social History.
C. Military History.
D. Political History.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Social History focuses on the "everyday lives, experiences, social structures, customs, and relationships of ordinary people in the past," often described as history "from the bottom up."

**5. Which of the following is an example of a primary source?**
A. A modern documentary about the Nigerian Civil War.
B. A history textbook chapter on the Oyo Empire.
C. A letter written by a soldier during World War II.
D. A biography of Kwame Nkrumah written in 2010.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** A primary source is an original, firsthand account created during the historical period being studied. The soldier's letter is a direct piece of evidence from that time.

**6. A history textbook is best categorized as a...**
A. Primary source.
B. Secondary source.
C. Oral tradition.
D. Tertiary source.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** A secondary source, like a textbook, is an interpretation of past events created by someone who was not a direct witness, based on an analysis of primary sources.

**7. A major limitation of oral traditions as historical sources is the...**
A. lack of cultural value.
B. difficulty in establishing precise chronology.
C. inability to provide insights into non-elite experiences.
D. reflection of the views of literate classes only.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The text notes that a key limitation of oral traditions is the "difficulty in establishing precise chronology," as events are not always tied to specific dates.

**8. The process of checking a historical source for authenticity to determine if it is a forgery is known as...**
A. Internal Criticism.
B. Contextualization.
C. External Criticism.
D. Corroboration.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** External Criticism is defined as the process of determining if a source is what it claims to be, checking for genuineness versus forgery or fabrication.

**9. The school of historiography that emphasizes class struggle and economic factors as the main drivers of historical change is...**
A. Nationalist Historiography.
B. Feminist Historiography.
C. Revisionist Historiography.
D. Marxist Historiography.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** Marxist Historiography, based on the theories of Karl Marx, focuses on economic factors, modes of production, and class struggle as the primary forces shaping history.

**10. An Afrocentric approach to historiography aims to...**
A. portray the past as an inevitable march of progress.
B. challenge Eurocentric biases by re-centering historical narratives from an African perspective.
C. focus exclusively on the history of European colonizers in Africa.
D. question the possibility of any objective historical truth.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Afrocentric Historiography's main goal is to challenge Eurocentric views that have marginalized African history, by focusing on African agency, civilizations, and perspectives.

---

### **Chapter 2: Early Civilizations in Africa (11-45)**

**11. The Nok civilization, one of the earliest complex societies in West Africa, is renowned for its...**
A. bronze plaques.
B. hieroglyphic writing.
C. terracotta sculptures.
D. massive pyramids.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Nok civilization is best known for its distinctive terracotta sculptures, which are among the oldest examples of figurative art in sub-Saharan Africa.

**12. A significant technological achievement of the Nok civilization was...**
A. the development of the lost-wax casting method.
B. the construction of large irrigation canals.
C. early iron smelting technology.
D. the invention of papyrus.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The text highlights that the Nok civilization provides evidence of early iron smelting, placing them among the earliest iron-using cultures in the region.

**13. The ancient city of Ife is considered the ancestral and spiritual homeland of the...**
A. Hausa people.
B. Edo people.
C. Akan people.
D. Yoruba people.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** Ife is explicitly identified in the text as the ancestral and spiritual homeland of the Yoruba people, and the cradle of their civilization.

**14. The magnificent bronze plaques depicting courtly life and historical events are a famous art form of the...**
A. Nok Civilization.
B. Kingdom of Benin.
C. Oyo Empire.
D. Kanem-Bornu Empire.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The Kingdom of Benin is famous for its magnificent bronze plaques, which richly documented life in the kingdom.

**15. The ruler of the Benin Kingdom was known by the title...**
A. Oni.
B. Alaafin.
C. Oba.
D. Mai.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Benin Kingdom was ruled by an Oba, who was considered a divine king with both political and spiritual authority.

**16. The political and spiritual leader of the Ife civilization was the...**
A. Oba.
B. Oni.
C. Sarki.
D. Alaafin.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The text states that Ife was ruled by an Oni, who was both a political and spiritual leader.

**17. Ancient Egyptian civilization was heavily dependent on the annual flooding of the...**
A. Niger River.
B. Congo River.
C. Zambezi River.
D. Nile River.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** The text quotes Herodotus, "Egypt is the gift of the Nile," emphasizing the river's central role in providing fertile silt for agriculture.

**18. The "Age of Pyramids" in Ancient Egypt, when the great pyramids of Giza were built, was during the...**
A. New Kingdom.
B. Old Kingdom.
C. Middle Kingdom.
D. Predynastic Period.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The Old Kingdom (c. 2686 – 2181 BC) is specifically referred to as the "Age of Pyramids," a time of strong central government and massive construction projects.

**19. The fundamental duty of the Egyptian pharaoh was to maintain cosmic order, justice, and harmony, a concept known as...**
A. Hieroglyph.
B. Papyrus.
C. Ma'at.
D. Osiris.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Ma'at was the crucial concept of cosmic order, truth, and justice that the pharaoh was responsible for maintaining.

**20. The primary purpose of the Egyptian pyramids was to serve as...**
A. temples for public worship.
B. astronomical observatories.
C. fortified granaries.
D. tombs for the pharaohs.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** The pyramids were massive stone monuments built primarily as tombs for pharaohs, designed to protect their bodies and provide for their journey to the afterlife.

**21. In Ancient Egyptian religion, the god of the underworld and resurrection was...**
A. Ra.
B. Horus.
C. Anubis.
D. Osiris.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** Osiris was the key deity associated with the underworld, death, and resurrection, central to Egyptian beliefs about the afterlife.

**22. The chief minister and second in command to the pharaoh in Ancient Egypt was the...**
A. Nomarch.
B. Scribe.
C. Vizier.
D. Priest.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Vizier was the chief minister who oversaw the entire administration of the state on behalf of the pharaoh.

**23. The most important animal used for transport in the Trans-Saharan trade was the...**
A. Horse.
B. Donkey.
C. Camel.
D. Ox.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Camels, known as "ships of the desert," were essential for the Trans-Saharan trade due to their ability to travel long distances without water and carry heavy loads.

**24. The most valuable commodity exported from West Africa across the Sahara was...**
A. Salt.
B. Cloth.
C. Gold.
D. Horses.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Gold, sourced from West African goldfields, was the major export and the primary driver of wealth for the Sudanic empires.

**25. A crucial commodity traded from the Sahara desert south into West Africa, sometimes ounce for ounce with gold, was...**
A. Ivory.
B. Slaves.
C. Salt.
D. Kola nuts.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Salt was an essential mineral that was scarce in West Africa but abundant in Saharan mines, making it extremely valuable in trade.

**26. One of the most significant long-term impacts of the Trans-Saharan trade on West Africa was the...**
A. introduction of pyramid building.
B. spread of Christianity.
C. decline of all major cities.
D. spread of Islam.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** Muslim traders from North Africa brought their faith along the trade routes, leading to the gradual spread of Islam among West African rulers, elites, and later the general population.

**27. The city of Timbuktu was a major southern terminus for the Trans-Saharan trade and a famous center for...**
A. bronze casting.
B. military training.
C. Islamic learning and scholarship.
D. terracotta sculpture.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Cities like Timbuktu became prosperous centers not only of trade but also of Islamic culture and learning, with famous mosques, universities, and libraries.

**28. The political organization of the Hausa states before the 19th century was characterized by...**
A. a single, unified empire.
B. independent city-states ruled by a Sarki.
C. a decentralized system with no rulers.
D. a council of elders with a temporary leader.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Hausaland was organized into several independent and often competing city-states, each ruled by a king known as a Sarki.

**29. The Fulani Jihad led by Usman dan Fodio in the early 19th century resulted in the...**
A. establishment of the Oyo Empire.
B. decline of the Trans-Saharan trade.
C. establishment of the Sokoto Caliphate.
D. conquest of the Benin Kingdom.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Usman dan Fodio's jihad overthrew most Hausa rulers and established the Sokoto Caliphate, a large Islamic empire that integrated the Hausa states under Fulani rule.

**30. The Kanem-Bornu Empire was centered around which major geographical feature?**
A. The Fouta Djallon highlands.
B. The Niger Delta.
C. The Jos Plateau.
D. Lake Chad.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** The Kanem-Bornu Empire, one of Africa's longest-lasting, was located around the basin of Lake Chad.

**31. The ruler of the Kanem-Bornu Empire held the title of...**
A. Alaafin.
B. Oba.
C. Mai.
D. Sarki.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Kanem-Bornu Empire was ruled by the Mai of the Sefuwa dynasty, one of the longest-ruling dynasties in history.

**32. The ruler of the Oyo Empire was known as the...**
A. Mai.
B. Sarki.
C. Oni.
D. Alaafin.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** The Oyo Empire, a powerful Yoruba state, was headed by the Alaafin, who was considered a divine ruler.

**33. In the Oyo Empire, the council of seven principal chiefs that served as a check on the Alaafin's power was the...**
A. Ogboni.
B. Sefuwa.
C. Oyo Mesi.
D. Uzama.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Oyo Mesi, led by the Bashorun, was a powerful council of kingmakers that could even depose the Alaafin, representing a key check and balance in the political system.

**34. The military strength of the Oyo Empire, which allowed for its expansion, was largely based on its...**
A. powerful navy.
B. use of firearms.
C. renowned cavalry.
D. large infantry of archers.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Oyo Empire was particularly renowned for its effective cavalry, which was a decisive factor in its military campaigns and expansion across the savanna.

**35. The lost-wax casting method, or *cire perdue*, was a sophisticated technique used in the art of...**
A. Nok and Ancient Egypt.
B. Ife and Benin.
C. Kanem-Bornu and Hausaland.
D. Nok and Oyo.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The highly naturalistic bronze and brass sculptures of both Ife and Benin were created using the advanced lost-wax casting method.

**36. The famous female pharaoh who ruled during the New Kingdom was...**
A. Cleopatra.
B. Nefertiti.
C. Hatshepsut.
D. Menkaure.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Hatshepsut was a notable female pharaoh of the New Kingdom who ruled for over two decades and oversaw major building projects.

**37. The Berber and Tuareg peoples played a crucial role in the Trans-Saharan trade as...**
A. gold miners.
B. salt producers.
C. kings of the Sudanic empires.
D. traders, guides, and controllers of oases.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** As nomadic inhabitants of the Sahara, the Berber and Tuareg were essential intermediaries who guided caravans, controlled vital oases, and conducted trade.

**38. The Bayajidda legend is the foundational myth that explains the origin of the...**
A. Oyo Empire.
B. Benin Kingdom.
C. Hausa states.
D. Kanem-Bornu Empire.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Bayajidda legend is the traditional story that accounts for the founding of the seven original Hausa states (Hausa Bakwai).

**39. The powerful secret society of elders that acted as a further check on the power of both the Alaafin and the Oyo Mesi was the...**
A. Uzama.
B. Ogboni.
C. NCNC.
D. Gwari.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The Ogboni society was a highly influential group involved in religious, judicial, and political matters that provided an additional check on the rulers of Oyo.

**40. The decline of the Trans-Saharan trade from the late 16th century was partly caused by the...**
A. exhaustion of salt mines.
B. rise of European maritime trade along the West African coast.
C. spread of Islam, which forbade trade.
D. collapse of the camel population.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The establishment of sea routes by Europeans shifted the focus of trade from the interior (Sahara) to the Atlantic coast, gradually undermining the Trans-Saharan networks.

**41. The largest of the Great Pyramids at Giza was built for Pharaoh...**
A. Djoser.
B. Tutankhamun.
C. Ramses II.
D. Khufu.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** The Great Pyramid of Giza, the largest of the three, was constructed as a tomb for the Fourth Dynasty pharaoh, Khufu.

**42. In the social hierarchy of Ancient Egypt, the majority of the population consisted of...**
A. Scribes.
B. Soldiers.
C. Farmers.
D. Slaves.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The vast majority of the Egyptian population were farmers who cultivated the land along the Nile, forming the base of the economy and society.

**43. The economic prosperity of the ancient Kingdom of Ghana (Wagadu) was largely derived from...**
A. its powerful navy.
B. its control over the Trans-Saharan trade routes, particularly in gold.
C. selling terracotta sculptures to North Africa.
D. large-scale cotton plantations.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The great Sudanic empires, starting with Ghana, derived immense wealth and power from controlling the sources of gold and taxing the Trans-Saharan trade that passed through their territory.

**44. The distinctive triangular or D-shaped eyes are a characteristic feature of the sculptures from...**
A. Ife.
B. Benin.
C. Nok.
D. Egypt.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Nok terracotta sculptures are known for their unique and stylized features, including triangular or D-shaped eyes and pierced pupils, nostrils, and mouths.

**45. Which of these West African civilizations is considered the oldest?**
A. Benin.
B. Ife.
C. Nok.
D. Oyo.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Nok civilization, flourishing from around 1500 BC, is significantly older than the civilizations of Ife, Benin, and Oyo.

---

### **Chapter 3: The Transatlantic and Trans-Saharan Slave Trades (46-70)**

**46. Before large-scale external demand, slavery in some African societies often resulted from...**
A. racial ideology.
B. industrial labor needs.
C. warfare, debt, or punishment for crimes.
D. the desire to populate new territories.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The text states that various forms of servitude existed in some African societies prior to the major external trades, with causes including capture in war, debt bondage, and judicial punishment.

**47. The "Middle Passage" refers to the...**
A. journey of enslaved Africans across the Sahara desert.
B. first leg of the triangular trade from Europe to Africa.
C. brutal sea voyage of enslaved Africans from Africa to the Americas.
D. overland route from the African interior to the coastal forts.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Middle Passage was the horrific second leg of the triangular trade, involving the forced transportation of millions of Africans across the Atlantic Ocean under inhumane conditions.

**48. What was the primary economic driver for the Transatlantic Slave Trade?**
A. The demand for domestic servants in Europe.
B. The demand for soldiers in Middle Eastern armies.
C. The demand for labor on plantations in the Americas.
D. The demand for artisans in African kingdoms.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The establishment of large-scale plantations for cash crops like sugar, cotton, and tobacco in the Americas created an immense demand for a large, exploitable labor force, which became the main driver of the Transatlantic Slave Trade.

**49. In the simplified model of the Triangular Trade, what was primarily transported from the Americas to Europe?**
A. Manufactured goods like guns and textiles.
B. Enslaved Africans.
C. Plantation products like sugar, cotton, and tobacco.
D. Gold and salt.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The third leg of the Triangular Trade involved ships returning to Europe with the valuable cash crops and raw materials produced by enslaved labor in the Americas.

**50. European slave traders on the West African coast most commonly acquired enslaved people by...**
A. directly raiding deep into the African interior themselves.
B. setting up their own plantations in Africa.
C. trading manufactured goods with African rulers and merchants who controlled the supply.
D. tricking people onto ships with promises of a better life.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** While direct raids occurred initially, the more common and larger-scale method was for Europeans to establish coastal forts and trade goods like guns, alcohol, and textiles in exchange for captives supplied by African intermediaries.

**51. A major social impact of the slave trade on West African societies was...**
A. a decrease in warfare and instability.
B. the strengthening of traditional family structures.
C. increased warfare and political instability.
D. a period of prolonged peace and prosperity.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The immense demand for captives fueled constant warfare, raids, and general insecurity as states and communities competed to capture people for the trade.

**52. The economic impact of the slave trade on West Africa included...**
A. the rapid industrialization of the region.
B. a shift away from agriculture towards manufacturing.
C. depopulation and the disruption of indigenous economies.
D. achieving economic independence from Europe.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The loss of millions of productive individuals led to depopulation, while economies were distorted to focus on slave raiding rather than balanced internal development, disrupting traditional farming and industries.

**53. Which religious group was among the earliest and most consistent opponents of the slave trade on moral grounds?**
A. The Anglicans.
B. The Catholics.
C. The Quakers (Society of Friends).
D. The Puritans.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The text identifies the Quakers as being among the earliest and most persistent critics of slavery, opposing it based on their religious and moral convictions.

**54. The successful slave revolt that led to the establishment of the first independent black republic and inspired abolitionists was in...**
A. Jamaica.
B. Brazil.
C. Haiti (Saint-Domingue).
D. Cuba.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Haitian Revolution (1791-1804), led by figures like Toussaint Louverture, resulted in the independent nation of Haiti and was a major blow to the institution of slavery.

**55. Which European nation was the first to abolish its slave trade in 1803?**
A. Great Britain.
B. France.
C. Portugal.
D. Denmark.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** Denmark holds the distinction of being the first European nation to pass legislation abolishing its transatlantic slave trade.

**56. Great Britain abolished the slave trade throughout its empire in...**
A. 1776.
B. 1807.
C. 1833.
D. 1865.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The British Parliament passed the Slave Trade Act in 1807, making the trade illegal for British subjects. The Royal Navy then played a role in suppressing the international trade.

**57. The abolition of slavery in the United States was achieved by the...**
A. Emancipation Proclamation in 1863.
B. Declaration of Independence in 1776.
C. 13th Amendment to the Constitution in 1865.
D. Missouri Compromise of 1820.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** While the Emancipation Proclamation freed slaves in Confederate territory, the 13th Amendment in 1865 formally and completely abolished slavery throughout the entire United States.

**58. The global dispersal of people of African descent, primarily due to the slave trade, is known as the...**
A. Great Migration.
B. African Diaspora.
C. Scramble for Africa.
D. Pan-African Movement.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The African Diaspora refers to the communities of people of African descent spread throughout the world as a result of forced and voluntary migration, with the slave trade being the largest cause.

**59. The development of racist ideologies was used to...**
A. encourage the abolition of slavery.
B. justify the enslavement of Africans.
C. promote equality between races.
D. explain the success of the Haitian Revolution.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Racist ideologies that dehumanized Africans were developed and promoted to provide a moral and intellectual justification for the brutal exploitation of the slave trade and slavery.

**60. Syncretic religions like Vodou in Haiti, Santería in Cuba, and Candomblé in Brazil are examples of...**
A. the complete erasure of African culture in the Americas.
B. cultural exchange and the blending of African spiritual beliefs with Christianity.
C. religions that were imported directly from Europe without change.
D. the failure of Christianity to attract any followers in the Americas.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** These religions are prime examples of cultural syncretism, where enslaved Africans and their descendants blended their traditional African spiritual systems with the Catholicism of their enslavers, creating new, unique faiths.

**61. The "Eric Williams thesis" controversially argued that the abolition of the slave trade was driven primarily by...**
A. moral and humanitarian concerns.
B. the declining profitability of the slave economy.
C. the success of slave revolts.
D. political pressure from the United States.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Historian Eric Williams posited that Britain abolished the slave trade not for humanitarian reasons, but because it was no longer as profitable and was becoming an economic liability.

**62. Which of the following goods were commonly traded from Europe to Africa in exchange for enslaved people?**
A. Gold, ivory, and salt.
B. Sugar, tobacco, and cotton.
C. Guns, alcohol, and textiles.
D. Kola nuts, hides, and ostrich feathers.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The first leg of the triangular trade involved European ships carrying manufactured goods, such as guns, alcohol, metalware, and cloth, to be exchanged on the African coast.

**63. The last country in the Americas to abolish slavery, in 1888, was...**
A. The United States.
B. Cuba.
C. Brazil.
D. Jamaica.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Brazil was the last nation in the Western Hemisphere to formally abolish the institution of slavery.

**64. The British policy of compensating slave owners, but not the enslaved, after the 1833 Slavery Abolition Act highlights...**
A. the success of the abolitionist movement.
B. the political and economic power held by the pro-slavery lobby.
C. the belief that slaves were not entitled to freedom.
D. the economic collapse of the British Empire.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The fact that compensation was paid to the owners of enslaved people (who were considered property) demonstrates the immense political influence they wielded and how abolition was negotiated to protect their financial interests.

**65. Key British abolitionist figures like William Wilberforce and Thomas Clarkson were heavily influenced by...**
A. Enlightenment philosophy and Evangelical Christian movements.
B. Marxist economic theory.
C. Nationalist sentiments.
D. Postmodernist thought.
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** The text highlights that the abolition movement was driven by a combination of Enlightenment ideas about human rights and the moral fervor of Evangelical Christian groups who saw slavery as unchristian.

**66. A significant demographic impact of the slave trade on some West African regions was...**
A. a population boom.
B. a balanced gender ratio.
C. a severe gender imbalance due to the preference for male captives.
D. an increase in the elderly population.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Because the trade often had a preference for young, able-bodied men for plantation labor, it led to a significant gender imbalance and demographic disruption in many societies that were raided for captives.

**67. African states like Dahomey and Asante...**
A. refused to participate in the slave trade.
B. were destroyed by the slave trade.
C. prospered temporarily by participating in and controlling the slave trade.
D. were the main drivers of the abolition movement in Africa.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The text notes that some African states and elites, such as those in Dahomey and Asante, became powerful and wealthy for a time by acting as intermediaries, waging war for captives, and controlling trade routes to the coast.

**68. The Trans-Saharan slave trade differed from the Transatlantic slave trade in that it...**
A. lasted for a much shorter period.
B. transported a larger number of people.
C. was driven by demand for plantation labor.
D. lasted for over a millennium and supplied slaves to North Africa and the Middle East.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** The Trans-Saharan trade was an ancient network that operated for over a thousand years, long before the Transatlantic trade, and was geared towards markets in North Africa and the Middle East for domestic, military, and other forms of labor.

**69. The introduction of firearms into West Africa through the slave trade had the effect of...**
A. promoting peace and stability.
B. discouraging local manufacturing.
C. intensifying warfare and slave-raiding activities.
D. helping Africans to defeat European colonizers.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Firearms became a key trade item that states needed to defend themselves and to capture more people. This created a vicious cycle, fueling an arms race and escalating conflicts.

**70. The historical memory and trauma of slavery continue to fuel contemporary discussions about...**
A. industrial development and urbanization.
B. reparations, racial justice, and identity.
C. the decline of traditional religions.
D. the success of colonial education.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The legacies of slavery, including systemic racism and economic inequality, are central to ongoing debates about reparations for historical injustice, the fight for racial equality, and the formation of identity in the African diaspora.

---

### **Chapter 4: European Colonization of Africa (71-100)**

**71. The intense period of competition among European powers to annex territory in Africa from c. 1880-1914 is known as the...**
A. Scramble for Africa.
B. triangular trade.
C. Berlin Conference.
D. partitioning of Africa.
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** The "Scramble for Africa" is the specific historical term for the rapid, competitive colonization of Africa by European powers in the late 19th and early 20th centuries.

**72. One of the primary economic motives for the European colonization of Africa was the...**
A. desire to spread African industrial techniques to Europe.
B. need to find new homes for Europe's excess population.
C. search for raw materials and new markets for manufactured goods.
D. philanthropic goal of distributing wealth to African states.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Industrial Revolution in Europe created a huge demand for raw materials (like rubber, cotton, and minerals) and a need for new markets to sell finished products, which were key economic drivers of colonialism.

**73. The "White Man's Burden" was an ideological justification for colonialism based on the belief in...**
A. economic equality.
B. European racial and cultural superiority.
C. African political sovereignty.
D. the separation of church and state.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** This concept, popularized by Rudyard Kipling, suggested that it was the duty of Europeans to "civilize" other peoples, a belief rooted in racist ideas of European superiority.

**74. The Berlin Conference of 1884-1885 was convened to...**
A. grant independence to African nations.
B. regulate European colonization and avoid conflicts among European powers.
C. form a military alliance against African resistance.
D. establish free trade agreements with African rulers.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The Berlin Conference was called by Otto von Bismarck to lay down the rules for the partition of Africa, with the primary goal of preventing the "scramble" from leading to a war between the colonizing powers.

**75. What was a major consequence of the boundary drawing at the Berlin Conference?**
A. It perfectly reflected existing African ethnic and political entities.
B. It created a unified African state.
C. It was done with the full participation of African leaders.
D. It created artificial boundaries that divided ethnic groups and grouped rivals together.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** The colonial boundaries were drawn arbitrarily by Europeans with no regard for the existing social, ethnic, or political landscape of Africa, leading to many post-colonial conflicts.

**76. The principle of "Effective Occupation" established at the Berlin Conference required European powers to...**
A. get permission from local rulers before claiming territory.
B. demonstrate administrative control on the ground to legitimize their claims.
C. pay for the land they claimed.
D. bring Christianity to the territory.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The principle of "Effective Occupation" meant that a claim on paper was not enough; a power had to show it had a real presence, such as administrators, troops, or forts, which accelerated the race to establish control.

**77. The British colonial administrative policy of ruling through existing traditional rulers and institutions was known as...**
A. Direct Rule.
B. Assimilation.
C. Indirect Rule.
D. Association.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Indirect Rule, most famously articulated by Lord Lugard, was the British system of using local chiefs and emirs as intermediaries to govern colonial subjects.

**78. The French colonial policy that aimed to absorb colonial subjects into French culture and turn them into "black Frenchmen" was known as...**
A. Indirect Rule.
B. Paternalism.
C. Association.
D. Assimilation.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** The French policy of "assimilation" was based on the idea of imbuing a select group of African elites with French culture, language, and values, granting them rights as citizens.

**79. Under colonial economic policies, African economies were primarily structured to...**
A. develop self-sufficiency and local industries.
B. serve the needs of the colonizing power by providing raw materials.
C. promote free trade among African states.
D. become major global manufacturing centers.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The primary goal of colonial economics was exploitation. Colonies were developed as sources of raw materials for European industries and as captive markets for European manufactured goods.

**80. Colonial governments often imposed hut and poll taxes on Africans primarily to...**
A. raise funds for African welfare programs.
B. force Africans to seek wage labor in colonial enterprises.
C. discourage population growth.
D. pay for the construction of mosques and traditional shrines.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** By requiring taxes to be paid in cash, colonial authorities forced Africans, who were often part of a subsistence economy, to work on European-owned farms, mines, or construction projects to earn the necessary money.

**81. The Mandinka leader who led a prolonged military resistance against French expansion in West Africa was...**
A. Yaa Asantewaa.
B. Menelik II.
C. Samori Ture.
D. Shaka Zulu.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Samori Ture led a sophisticated and determined military resistance against the French for over 15 years, making him a key figure of primary resistance.

**82. The War of the Golden Stool in 1900 was an uprising led by Yaa Asantewaa of the Asante against the...**
A. French.
B. Germans.
C. Portuguese.
D. British.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** Yaa Asantewaa led the Asante in their final major war against the British after the British governor demanded possession of the Golden Stool, the symbol of the Asante nation.

**83. Which African nation successfully defeated a European power (Italy) at the Battle of Adwa in 1896, thereby preserving its independence?**
A. Egypt.
B. Ethiopia.
C. Ghana.
D. Nigeria.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Ethiopia, under Emperor Menelik II, inflicted a decisive defeat on the invading Italian army at Adwa, ensuring it remained one of the only African nations to avoid European colonization.

**84. A major negative effect of colonialism on African political structures was the...**
A. introduction of universal democracy.
B. strengthening of traditional checks and balances.
C. loss of political independence and sovereignty.
D. complete elimination of ethnic identities.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The most fundamental political effect of colonialism was the subjugation of African peoples and the loss of their right to self-governance.

**85. Colonial infrastructure, like railways and roads, was primarily built to...**
A. facilitate integrated national development and link African communities.
B. promote tourism within the colonies.
C. facilitate the extraction of resources and movement of troops.
D. create jobs for unemployed Europeans.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The layout of colonial infrastructure was strategic, not developmental. It was designed to get raw materials from the interior to the ports for export and to enable the colonial state to assert military control.

**86. The introduction of Western education in the colonies, though limited, ironically...**
A. made Africans more supportive of colonial rule.
B. created an educated elite that would later lead nationalist movements.
C. completely destroyed traditional African knowledge systems.
D. led to widespread economic prosperity for all Africans.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Colonial education exposed a small class of Africans to Western ideas of democracy, liberty, and self-determination, which they then used to argue for independence and lead anti-colonial movements.

**87. A system of forced or conscripted labor was often used by colonial powers for...**
A. missionary work.
B. public works projects like road and railway construction.
C. teaching Europeans local languages.
D. electing local chiefs.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Colonial governments frequently used forced labor to build the infrastructure they needed, compelling Africans to work under harsh conditions for little or no pay.

**88. The colonial practice of favoring one ethnic group over others for roles in the military or administration is an example of...**
A. assimilation policy.
B. indirect rule.
C. civilizing mission.
D. divide and rule tactics.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** "Divide and rule" was a common strategy where colonizers would exploit existing tensions or create new ones by favoring certain ethnic groups, thereby making it harder for a united resistance to form.

**89. The Maji Maji Rebellion (1905-1907) was a major uprising against colonial rule in...**
A. German East Africa.
B. French West Africa.
C. British Nigeria.
D. Belgian Congo.
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** The Maji Maji Rebellion was a large-scale armed revolt by several indigenous communities in German East Africa (modern-day Tanzania) against the forced cultivation of cotton and other harsh colonial policies.

**90. The main difference between Direct and Indirect Rule was the...**
A. level of economic exploitation.
B. role assigned to traditional rulers.
C. type of cash crops grown.
D. presence of Christian missionaries.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The key distinction lay in how traditional leaders were utilized. Under Indirect Rule, they were incorporated into the colonial administration as intermediaries, while under Direct Rule, their power was largely bypassed in favor of European officials.

**91. The Maxim gun was a key technological advantage that enabled...**
A. African resistance against colonialism.
B. the rapid European conquest of the African interior.
C. the building of pyramids in Egypt.
D. the Trans-Saharan trade to flourish.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Superior weaponry, particularly the Maxim machine gun, gave European armies a decisive military advantage over most African forces, facilitating the conquest.

**92. Which two countries remained independent of direct European rule by 1914?**
A. Egypt and South Africa.
B. Ghana and Nigeria.
C. Ethiopia and Liberia.
D. Algeria and Morocco.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Ethiopia successfully resisted Italian invasion, and Liberia, founded by freed American slaves, maintained its independence through its historical ties to the United States.

**93. The economic system where colonies produce cash crops for export while importing manufactured goods from the colonizing country leads to...**
A. economic self-sufficiency.
B. rapid industrialization.
C. a balanced and diversified economy.
D. economic dependency.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** This structure created a relationship of dependency, where the colony's economy was reliant on fluctuating world prices for its few exports and dependent on the colonizer for finished products.

**94. What was Lord Lugard's primary rationale for advocating Indirect Rule in Northern Nigeria?**
A. It was a step towards immediate independence.
B. It was more cost-effective and pragmatic in a region with strong, existing states.
C. It was the best way to introduce Christianity.
D. The French were already using this system successfully.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Lugard saw Indirect Rule as a practical and cheaper way to govern vast territories with few European officials by co-opting the established and sophisticated political structures of the Sokoto Caliphate.

**95. A cultural effect of colonialism was the...**
A. promotion of all African languages to official status.
B. strengthening of traditional religious practices.
C. imposition of European languages, religion, and values, often denigrating indigenous cultures.
D. complete integration of European and African cultural norms.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Colonialism was a form of cultural imperialism, where the colonizer's culture was presented as superior and indigenous cultures were often suppressed or undermined through education, missionary work, and administration.

**96. Land alienation was a particularly severe problem in settler colonies like...**
A. Nigeria and Ghana.
B. Kenya and Zimbabwe.
C. Senegal and Guinea.
D. Benin and Togo.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** In colonies where large numbers of Europeans settled permanently, such as Kenya, Zimbabwe (Southern Rhodesia), and South Africa, vast tracts of the most fertile land were expropriated from Africans for the settlers.

**97. The Hut Tax War of 1898 was a response to British taxation policies in...**
A. Ghana.
B. Nigeria.
C. Sierra Leone.
D. The Gambia.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Hut Tax War was a significant armed resistance in the protectorate of Sierra Leone, led by Bai Bureh, against the imposition of a new tax on dwellings by the British.

**98. The primary aim of French assimilation policy was to create...**
A. a federal system of government.
B. an elite class of Africans who were culturally French.
C. economic equality between France and its colonies.
D. independent African states allied with France.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Assimilation was designed to transform a small, educated elite into "évolués" (evolved ones) who adopted French language, culture, and laws, thereby creating loyal intermediaries.

**99. Which of the following best describes the legacy of colonial boundaries in Africa?**
A. They created stable and unified nation-states.
B. They became a source of many post-independence ethnic conflicts and political instability.
C. They were redrawn by African leaders immediately after independence.
D. They successfully separated all rival ethnic groups.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The artificial boundaries that ignored ethnic realities have been a persistent source of tension, civil wars, and challenges to national unity in the post-colonial era.

**100. The concept of "neo-colonialism" suggests that after political independence, African nations...**
A. achieved full economic and political freedom.
B. chose to be re-colonized by European powers.
C. remained economically and politically dependent on external powers.
D. became the dominant powers in the global economy.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Neo-colonialism is the theory that former colonial powers and other external forces continue to exert indirect control over politically independent nations, especially through economic, financial, and trade mechanisms.

---

### **Chapter 5: Struggle for Independence in West Africa (101-130)**

**101. A major factor contributing to the rise of nationalism in West Africa after World War II was the...**
A. economic prosperity brought by colonial rule.
B. experience of African soldiers who fought for colonial powers and the ideals of the Atlantic Charter.
C. decision by colonial powers to grant independence voluntarily.
D. success of the Berlin Conference.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** African soldiers' exposure to the world and the hypocrisy of fighting for freedom while being denied it, combined with the Allied promise of self-determination, greatly fueled nationalist aspirations.

**102. The intellectual movement advocating for the unity and self-determination of all people of African descent, which influenced many nationalist leaders, was known as...**
A. Pan-Africanism.
B. Nationalism.
C. Socialism.
D. Communism.
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** Pan-Africanism was a crucial ideological force that brought together leaders from across Africa and the diaspora, inspiring them with a vision of a free and united continent.

**103. The United Gold Coast Convention (UGCC) initially advocated for...**
A. "Self-Government Now!"
B. immediate withdrawal of all British personnel.
C. "self-government within the shortest possible time."
D. the establishment of a one-party state.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The UGCC, formed by the educated elite, had a more moderate and gradualist approach compared to the radical demand for immediate self-government later championed by Nkrumah.

**104. Kwame Nkrumah broke away from the UGCC to form which more radical, mass-based political party?**
A. The National Council of Nigeria and the Cameroons (NCNC).
B. The Action Group (AG).
C. The Convention People's Party (CPP).
D. The Northern People's Congress (NPC).
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Nkrumah formed the CPP in 1949 to mobilize the masses with the radical slogan "Self-Government Now!", a direct challenge to the UGCC's gradualism.

**105. "Positive Action," a strategy of non-violent civil disobedience, strikes, and boycotts, was championed by...**
A. Obafemi Awolowo.
B. Nnamdi Azikiwe.
C. Ahmadu Bello.
D. Kwame Nkrumah.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** Nkrumah and the CPP adopted "Positive Action" as their main strategy to put pressure on the British colonial administration to grant immediate independence.

**106. The three major nationalist political parties in Nigeria on the eve of independence were largely based on...**
A. religious affiliations.
B. economic class.
C. regional and ethnic loyalties.
D. professional associations.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The NCNC (East/Igbo), AG (West/Yoruba), and NPC (North/Hausa-Fulani) were primarily based in the three major regions of Nigeria and drew their support from the dominant ethnic groups in those regions.

**107. The leader of the Action Group (AG) and Premier of the Western Region of Nigeria was...**
A. Nnamdi Azikiwe.
B. Obafemi Awolowo.
C. Herbert Macaulay.
D. Abubakar Tafawa Balewa.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Obafemi Awolowo was the founder and leader of the AG and a key advocate for federalism and welfarist policies in the Western Region.

**108. The National Council of Nigeria and the Cameroons (NCNC) was led by the prominent nationalist...**
A. Ahmadu Bello.
B. Obafemi Awolowo.
C. Nnamdi Azikiwe.
D. Milton Margai.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Nnamdi "Zik" Azikiwe, a journalist and eloquent orator, became the leader of the NCNC and a central figure in the fight for Nigerian independence.

**109. The Northern People's Congress (NPC) in Nigeria was led by...**
A. Kwame Nkrumah.
B. J.B. Danquah.
C. Ahmadu Bello.
D. Herbert Macaulay.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Ahmadu Bello, the Sardauna of Sokoto, was the influential leader of the NPC, which represented the interests of the Northern Region.

**110. Which West African nation became the first in sub-Saharan Africa to gain independence from colonial rule?**
A. Nigeria.
B. Sierra Leone.
C. Ghana.
D. The Gambia.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Ghana, formerly the Gold Coast, achieved independence under the leadership of Kwame Nkrumah on March 6, 1957, a landmark event for the entire continent.

**111. Nigeria gained its independence on...**
A. March 6, 1957.
B. October 1, 1960.
C. April 27, 1961.
D. February 18, 1965.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Nigeria achieved independence from Great Britain on October 1, 1960.

**112. The first Prime Minister of independent Nigeria was...**
A. Nnamdi Azikiwe.
B. Obafemi Awolowo.
C. Abubakar Tafawa Balewa.
D. Ahmadu Bello.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Following the 1959 elections, a coalition government was formed, and Abubakar Tafawa Balewa of the NPC became Nigeria's first and only Prime Minister.

**113. The first President of a republican Nigeria (a ceremonial role) was...**
A. Abubakar Tafawa Balewa.
B. Nnamdi Azikiwe.
C. Obafemi Awolowo.
D. Herbert Macaulay.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** When Nigeria became a republic in 1963, Nnamdi Azikiwe, who had been Governor-General, became the country's first President, a largely ceremonial position at the time.

**114. The French colony that uniquely voted "No" to remaining in the French Community in 1958, resulting in immediate and punitive independence, was...**
A. Senegal.
B. Ivory Coast.
C. Guinea.
D. Mali.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Under the leadership of Sékou Touré, Guinea was the only French colony to reject Charles de Gaulle's proposal, choosing complete and immediate independence. France responded by abruptly withdrawing all aid and personnel.

**115. A major challenge faced by newly independent West African nations was...**
A. a surplus of experienced political leaders.
B. forging a sense of national unity among diverse ethnic groups.
C. deciding how to return to colonial rule.
D. managing their over-industrialized economies.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Nation-building was a primary challenge, as leaders had to create a shared national identity to overcome the ethnic and regional loyalties that were often stronger than loyalty to the new state created by colonial boundaries.

**116. The poet, philosopher, and political leader who was a key figure in the Negritude movement and the first President of Senegal was...**
A. Sékou Touré.
B. Félix Houphouët-Boigny.
C. Léopold Sédar Senghor.
D. J.B. Danquah.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Léopold Sédar Senghor was a towering intellectual figure, a proponent of the Negritude cultural movement, and the first president of independent Senegal.

**117. Who is often referred to as the "father of Nigerian nationalism" for his early advocacy work?**
A. Nnamdi Azikiwe.
B. Obafemi Awolowo.
C. Herbert Macaulay.
D. Ahmadu Bello.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Herbert Macaulay was a pioneer nationalist who founded the Nigerian National Democratic Party (NNDP) in 1923 and was a leading voice for Nigerian rights long before the post-WWII movements.

**118. The West African Students' Union (WASU) was an important early nationalist organization that...**
A. led armed resistance against the British.
B. functioned as a political party in Ghana.
C. provided a forum for students in London to discuss colonialism and advocate for West African interests.
D. organized the Berlin Conference.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** WASU, based in London, was a crucial training ground for future nationalist leaders, where they could organize, debate ideas, and petition the colonial authorities.

**119. The leader of the Sierra Leone People's Party (SLPP) and the first Prime Minister of Sierra Leone was...**
A. Siaka Stevens.
B. Kwame Nkrumah.
C. Dawda Jawara.
D. Sir Milton Margai.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** Sir Milton Margai, a medical doctor, founded the SLPP and led Sierra Leone to a peaceful independence in 1961, becoming its first prime minister.

**120. A key economic challenge for post-independence African nations was...**
A. an over-diversified economy.
B. an over-developed industrial base.
C. inherited colonial economies dependent on the export of a few raw materials.
D. a lack of trading partners.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** New nations inherited economies designed for colonial exploitation, heavily reliant on one or two cash crops or minerals, making them vulnerable to global price fluctuations and subject to neo-colonialism.

**121. The 1945 Pan-African Congress, which was a major catalyst for post-war nationalism, was held in...**
A. Accra.
B. London.
C. Manchester.
D. Paris.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The 1945 Manchester Pan-African Congress was a pivotal event, attended by figures like Nkrumah, which moved from demanding reforms to demanding outright independence.

**122. The Action Group's policy of free primary education in the Western Region of Nigeria is an example of...**
A. a nationalist party's welfarist agenda.
B. a colonial directive.
C. a failed economic plan.
D. the enforcement of a one-party state.
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** This policy, championed by Awolowo's AG, demonstrates how nationalist parties sought to bring tangible social development and welfare benefits to their people, a key part of their political platform.

**123. The Rassemblement Démocratique Africain (RDA) was a major nationalist movement in...**
A. British West Africa.
B. French West Africa.
C. German West Africa.
D. Portuguese West Africa.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The RDA was an influential inter-territorial political movement that had branches across the colonies of French West Africa, coordinating nationalist efforts.

**124. What was the main difference in strategy between the UGCC and the CPP in Ghana?**
A. The UGCC was violent, while the CPP was non-violent.
B. The UGCC wanted gradual reform, while the CPP demanded immediate self-government.
C. The UGCC was socialist, while the CPP was capitalist.
D. The UGCC was based in the North, while the CPP was based in the South.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The fundamental split was over timing and approach: the elite-led UGCC sought a slow, negotiated path, while Nkrumah's mass-based CPP demanded "Self-Government Now!"

**125. Which of the following was NOT a major challenge in the early post-independence era?**
A. Nation-building and ethnic tensions.
B. Economic development and neo-colonialism.
C. Political instability and military coups.
D. A surplus of capital and skilled manpower.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** A shortage, not a surplus, of capital and skilled professionals was a major obstacle to development for newly independent nations.

**126. The British colonial constitution that first introduced the elective principle in Nigeria was the...**
A. Richards Constitution of 1946.
B. Macpherson Constitution of 1951.
C. Clifford Constitution of 1922.
D. Lyttelton Constitution of 1954.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Clifford Constitution of 1922 was significant for introducing the elective principle, allowing for the election of a few African members to the legislative council, mainly in Lagos and Calabar.

**127. The leader who maintained very close ties with France after his country's independence was...**
A. Sékou Touré of Guinea.
B. Kwame Nkrumah of Ghana.
C. Félix Houphouët-Boigny of Ivory Coast.
D. Modibo Keïta of Mali.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Unlike the radical break made by Sékou Touré, Houphouët-Boigny of Ivory Coast pursued a policy of close political and economic cooperation with France after independence.

**128. The formation of regional political parties in Nigeria ahead of independence was a direct consequence of the...**
A. Richards Constitution, which emphasized regionalism.
B. policy of indirect rule.
C. success of the CPP in Ghana.
D. outcomes of the Nigerian Civil War.
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** The Richards Constitution of 1946 divided Nigeria into three administrative regions, which inadvertently promoted regional rather than national political consciousness and led to the formation of parties based on these regional-ethnic lines.

**129. The independence of India in 1947 was significant for African nationalism because it...**
A. provided direct financial support to African parties.
B. proved that a major colonial power, Britain, was willing to decolonize.
C. led to the formation of the United Nations.
D. ended the Cold War.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** India's independence served as a powerful inspiration and a precedent, showing African nationalists that independence from a major European empire was an achievable goal.

**130. The term 'neo-colonialism' was popularized by which West African leader to describe the continued economic dominance by former colonial powers?**
A. Obafemi Awolowo.
B. Kwame Nkrumah.
C. Nnamdi Azikiwe.
D. Léopold Sédar Senghor.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Kwame Nkrumah was a leading critic of neo-colonialism, writing extensively on how politically independent African states remained economically subservient to Western interests.

---

### **Chapter 6: Political Developments Since Independence (131-165)**

**131. A key feature of Kwame Nkrumah's government in Ghana after 1960 was its...**
A. multi-party democracy and regional autonomy.
B. strong advocacy for African unity and Pan-Africanism.
C. close alliance with former colonial powers.
D. free-market capitalist economic policies.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Nkrumah was a foremost champion of Pan-Africanism, making Accra a hub for liberation movements and advocating for a "United States of Africa."

**132. The Preventive Detention Act of 1958 in Ghana allowed Nkrumah's government to...**
A. nationalize foreign companies.
B. detain individuals without trial.
C. establish a one-party state.
D. create the Akosombo Dam.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The Preventive Detention Act was a key piece of legislation that marked Ghana's shift towards authoritarianism, as it was used to imprison political opponents without due process.

**133. In 1964, Ghana under Kwame Nkrumah officially became a...**
A. federal republic.
B. constitutional monarchy.
C. multi-party state.
D. one-party state.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** In 1964, following a referendum, the constitution was amended to make the Convention People's Party (CPP) the sole legal party and Nkrumah the Life President.

**134. Kwame Nkrumah was overthrown in a military coup in 1966 while he was on a state visit to...**
A. the United States and Britain.
B. Nigeria and Togo.
C. the Soviet Union and East Germany.
D. China and Vietnam.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** Nkrumah's overthrow occurred in February 1966 while he was abroad on a peace mission to North Vietnam and China.

**135. The First Republic of Nigeria (1960-1966) was characterized by...**
A. political stability and national unity.
B. military rule.
C. a one-party system.
D. intense ethnic regionalism and political rivalry.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** The First Republic was plagued by instability arising from the fierce competition for power and resources among the three major regions and their dominant ethnic groups.

**136. The first military coup in Nigeria took place on...**
A. October 1, 1960.
B. January 15, 1966.
C. July 29, 1966.
D. May 30, 1967.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The civilian government of the First Republic was overthrown on January 15, 1966, by a group of young army majors.

**137. The immediate cause of the Nigerian Civil War was the...**
A. military coup of January 1966.
B. discovery of oil in the Niger Delta.
C. secession of the Eastern Region as the Republic of Biafra.
D. annulment of the 1964 federal election.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The declaration of independence by the Eastern Region, led by Lt. Col. Ojukwu, in May 1967, was the act of secession that triggered the federal government's military response, leading to war.

**138. The Nigerian Civil War was fought between...**
A. 1960 and 1963.
B. 1966 and 1968.
C. 1967 and 1970.
D. 1975 and 1979.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The war lasted for approximately 30 months, from July 1967 to January 1970.

**139. The Nigerian Head of State during the Civil War was...**
A. Johnson Aguiyi-Ironsi.
B. Yakubu Gowon.
C. Murtala Muhammed.
D. Olusegun Obasanjo.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** General Yakubu Gowon led the Federal Military Government of Nigeria throughout the civil war and declared the policy of "No Victor, No Vanquished" at its end.

**140. The leader of the secessionist state of Biafra was...**
A. Yakubu Gowon.
B. Nnamdi Azikiwe.
C. Chukwuemeka Odumegwu Ojukwu.
D. Philip Effiong.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Lt. Colonel Ojukwu, the military governor of the Eastern Region, led the Biafran secession and was its head of state throughout the war.

**141. The policy of Reconciliation, Reconstruction, and Reabsorption (3 Rs) was initiated in Nigeria by...**
A. the Ojukwu regime.
B. the Gowon regime.
C. the Shagari administration.
D. the Buhari regime.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** After the Biafran surrender, Yakubu Gowon's government announced the "3 Rs" policy to promote healing and reintegrate the former secessionist region back into Nigeria.

**142. The annulment of the June 12, 1993 presidential election in Nigeria, presumed to have been won by M.K.O. Abiola, occurred under the military regime of...**
A. Muhammadu Buhari.
B. Ibrahim Babangida.
C. Sani Abacha.
D. Abdulsalami Abubakar.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** General Ibrahim Babangida's regime annulled the results of the 1993 election, a move that plunged Nigeria into a prolonged political crisis.

**143. The military regime of Sani Abacha in Nigeria (1993-1998) is best remembered for being...**
A. a period of democratic transition.
B. highly authoritarian and repressive.
C. a time of great economic prosperity.
D. focused on reconciliation and unity.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The Abacha regime was known for its brutality, widespread human rights abuses, execution of activists like Ken Saro-Wiwa, and international isolation.

**144. Nigeria's return to civilian democratic rule in 1999, marking the start of the Fourth Republic, was overseen by the military head of state...**
A. Sani Abacha.
B. Ibrahim Babangida.
C. Abdulsalami Abubakar.
D. Olusegun Obasanjo.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Following Abacha's sudden death, General Abdulsalami Abubakar took power and initiated a swift transition program that led to democratic elections and the handover of power in 1999.

**145. The first elected President of Nigeria's Fourth Republic was...**
A. Shehu Shagari.
B. M.K.O. Abiola.
C. Olusegun Obasanjo.
D. Umaru Musa Yar'Adua.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Olusegun Obasanjo, a former military head of state, was elected as the first civilian president of the Fourth Republic in 1999.

**146. A common justification cited by military leaders for staging coups in West Africa was...**
A. the need for closer ties with colonial powers.
B. a desire to implement communist policies.
C. civilian government corruption and mismanagement.
D. a lack of resources for the military.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Military plotters almost invariably justified their takeovers by pointing to the corruption, political infighting, election rigging, and economic failures of the civilian governments they overthrew.

**147. ECOWAS is primarily an organization for...**
A. military alliance.
B. cultural exchange.
C. economic integration in West Africa.
D. religious harmony.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS) was founded with the primary goal of promoting economic cooperation and integration among its member states.

**148. The peacekeeping and intervention force of ECOWAS is known as...**
A. ECOMOG.
B. OAU.
C. AU.
D. APSA.
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** ECOMOG (ECOWAS Monitoring Group) is the military intervention wing of the organization, famously deployed in the civil wars in Liberia and Sierra Leone.

**149. The African Union (AU) replaced which older continental organization in 2002?**
A. The United Nations (UN).
B. The Non-Aligned Movement (NAM).
C. The Organization of African Unity (OAU).
D. The Commonwealth of Nations.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The AU was established to succeed the OAU, with a stronger mandate for peace, security, and intervention in member states' affairs.

**150. A key principle of the African Union (AU) that differs from the OAU's strict non-interference policy is the...**
A. right to colonize other member states.
B. policy of non-indifference, allowing for intervention in grave circumstances.
C. requirement for all member states to use a single currency.
D. focus on military coups as the only legitimate form of government change.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The AU's Constitutive Act embraces the principle of "non-indifference," which gives the Union the right to intervene in a member state in cases of war crimes, genocide, and crimes against humanity, a significant shift from the OAU's strict non-interference.

**151. The Akosombo Dam, a major development project, was undertaken in...**
A. Nigeria.
B. Ghana.
C. Sierra Leone.
D. Ivory Coast.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The Volta River Project, which included the construction of the Akosombo Dam for hydroelectric power, was a cornerstone of Kwame Nkrumah's industrialization and development plan for Ghana.

**152. The Unification Decree of 1966, which abolished Nigeria's federal system, was enacted by...**
A. Yakubu Gowon.
B. Johnson Aguiyi-Ironsi.
C. Tafawa Balewa.
D. Murtala Muhammed.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** General Aguiyi-Ironsi's Decree No. 34 sought to unify the country by abolishing the regions and instituting a unitary system of government, a move that was deeply unpopular, especially in the North, and contributed to the counter-coup.

**153. A major factor that contributed to the economic problems of Nigeria's Second Republic (1979-1983) was...**
A. a boom in agricultural prices.
B. a sharp fall in global oil prices.
C. the discovery of massive gold reserves.
D. the closure of all international trade.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The civilian government of Shehu Shagari was beset by economic challenges, significantly worsened by a global oil glut and a collapse in oil prices, which was Nigeria's main source of revenue.

**154. The ECOWAS Protocol on Democracy and Good Governance condemns...**
A. multi-party elections.
B. unconstitutional changes of government.
C. free-market economies.
D. Pan-Africanism.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** This protocol sets standards for democratic rule among member states and explicitly condemns and provides for sanctions against unconstitutional takeovers like military coups.

**155. The controversial 1962/63 census in Nigeria was a major source of political tension because...**
A. it was conducted by the British.
B. it showed the population was declining.
C. the results would determine the allocation of parliamentary seats and resources.
D. it revealed that the Igbo were the largest ethnic group.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** In a country where political power was based on population size, the census results were crucial for determining regional representation and resource allocation, making the controversial figures a major point of conflict.

**156. The term "Third Republic" in Nigeria often refers to...**
A. the period of the Civil War.
B. the democratic structures set up during Babangida's transition that were never fully realized.
C. the current democratic dispensation since 1999.
D. the first military regime under Gowon.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The Third Republic is the term for the ill-fated democratic experiment under General Babangida, which was aborted with the annulment of the 1993 election.

**157. The economic Structural Adjustment Program (SAP) was implemented in Nigeria under the regime of...**
A. Shehu Shagari.
B. Muhammadu Buhari.
C. Ibrahim Babangida.
D. Sani Abacha.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Babangida regime implemented the IMF/World Bank-backed SAP, which involved measures like currency devaluation and removal of subsidies, leading to significant economic hardship and public protest.

**158. The Peace and Security Council (PSC) is the main organ for conflict resolution within the...**
A. United Nations.
B. European Union.
C. African Union.
D. ECOWAS.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Peace and Security Council (PSC) is the AU's standing decision-making body for the prevention, management, and resolution of conflicts in Africa.

**159. "War Against Indiscipline" (WAI) was a social engineering campaign aimed at promoting order and civic responsibility in Nigeria under the regime of...**
A. Gowon.
B. Obasanjo (military).
C. Buhari (military).
D. Babangida.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The military government of Muhammadu Buhari (1983-1985) was known for its strict WAI campaign, which enforced queuing, environmental sanitation, and other civic virtues.

**160. Which of the following is an example of a successful ECOWAS intervention to restore democratic rule?**
A. The intervention in the Nigerian Civil War.
B. The pressure on Yahya Jammeh to step down after losing the 2016 election in The Gambia.
C. The support for Kwame Nkrumah's one-party state.
D. The prevention of the Scramble for Africa.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** ECOWAS played a decisive role by threatening military intervention, which successfully pressured President Jammeh of The Gambia to accept his election defeat and hand over power peacefully in early 2017.

**161. A "cult of personality" refers to the creation of an idealized, heroic, and worshipful image of a leader. This was a feature of the government of...**
A. Abubakar Tafawa Balewa in Nigeria.
B. Kwame Nkrumah in Ghana.
C. Dawda Jawara in The Gambia.
D. Milton Margai in Sierra Leone.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** As Nkrumah's rule became more authoritarian, a significant cult of personality developed around him, with his image and name (Osagyefo - 'the redeemer') becoming ubiquitous.

**162. The political crisis in Nigeria's Western Region in the 1960s, known as "Operation Wetie", was a violent conflict involving supporters of the...**
A. NPC and NCNC.
B. AG and NNDP (a breakaway faction).
C. CPP and UGCC.
D. Federal Government and Biafra.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The crisis stemmed from a split within the Action Group (AG) and intense, violent rivalry between the mainstream AG and a new party, the NNDP, which was supported by the federal government, leading to widespread chaos.

**163. The creation of more states in Nigeria, starting from 12 states in 1967, was a strategy by the federal government to...**
A. increase the power of the regions.
B. break the power of the major ethnic groups and address minority fears.
C. prepare the country for secession.
D. make the country easier for Britain to control.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Gowon's creation of 12 states from the four regions was a masterstroke that aimed to undermine the basis of the Biafran secession (by creating states for minorities within the Eastern region) and address the fears of minority groups across the country.

**164. The primary commodity that fueled Nigeria's economy and also its political conflicts after the civil war was...**
A. Cocoa.
B. Palm oil.
C. Coal.
D. Petroleum (Oil).
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** The oil boom of the 1970s made petroleum the dominant source of Nigeria's wealth, leading to intense competition among different groups and regions for control over the revenue it generated.

**165. The African Peer Review Mechanism (APRM) is an initiative of the African Union designed to...**
A. review military strategies.
B. encourage self-monitoring and good governance among member states.
C. provide financial loans to member states.
D. observe elections only.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The APRM is a voluntary mechanism where member states agree to be reviewed by their peers on their performance in areas of political, economic, and corporate governance.

---

### **Chapter 7: Traditional Societies, Religion, and Culture (166-190)**

**166. A kinship system where descent and inheritance are traced through the male line is known as...**
A. Matrilineal.
B. Bilateral.
C. Patrilineal.
D. Avuncular.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Patrilineal descent, which traces lineage through the father's side, is a common kinship system in many West African societies.

**167. An age grade system is a social organization based on...**
A. wealth and status.
B. religious affiliation.
C. age, where individuals pass through life stages together.
D. kinship and lineage.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Age grades and age sets are formal institutions that group people of similar age together, assigning them collective roles and responsibilities that change as they mature.

**168. In many traditional West African political systems, the power of a chief or king was often checked by...**
A. a foreign colonial power.
B. a written constitution.
C. a council of elders or other societal institutions.
D. a neighboring rival kingdom.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Rulers were rarely absolute. Their power was balanced by institutions like councils of elders, kingmakers (like the Oyo Mesi), or secret societies (like the Ogboni), which could advise and even depose them.

**169. In many indigenous African religions, the Supreme Being is often seen as...**
A. directly involved in everyday human affairs.
B. a remote creator, with worship directed through lesser divinities or ancestors.
C. a malevolent and punishing force.
D. one of many equal gods.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** A common feature is the belief in a single High God or Creator who is powerful but distant, with more immediate spiritual interaction occurring through intermediaries like deities and ancestors.

**170. The veneration of ancestors in traditional African religions is based on the belief that...**
A. ancestors are evil spirits to be feared.
B. deceased relatives continue to influence the lives of the living.
C. all dead people automatically become gods.
D. the living have no connection to the past.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Ancestor veneration stems from the belief that ancestors exist in the spirit world and act as intermediaries who can provide protection and guidance to their living descendants.

**171. The practice of pouring libations is a common ritual for...**
A. declaring war.
B. honoring ancestors and spirits.
C. choosing a new chief.
D. celebrating the harvest of cash crops.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Pouring libations (a drink offering) on the ground is a widespread ritual act of respect, prayer, and communion with ancestors and spiritual entities.

**172. Divination, such as the Ifa system among the Yoruba, is a practice used to...**
A. punish criminals in the community.
B. gain insight into problems, communicate with spirits, and understand the future.
C. elect a new king.
D. teach children how to read and write.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Divination is a complex spiritual practice where a trained specialist (diviner) uses specific techniques to interpret the will of the gods or spirits, diagnose misfortunes, and prescribe solutions.

**173. Islam was first introduced into West Africa primarily through...**
A. European missionaries.
B. military conquest from the sea.
C. North African traders and scholars along the Trans-Saharan routes.
D. the Atlantic slave trade.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Islam spread peacefully into West Africa over many centuries, carried by Arab and Berber merchants and clerics who traveled with the trans-Saharan caravans.

**174. The establishment of Islamic centers of learning, such as in Timbuktu, was a major impact of...**
A. Christianity in West Africa.
B. Colonial education policies.
C. The spread of Islam in the Sudanic empires.
D. Indigenous religious practices.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The adoption of Islam by rulers of empires like Mali and Songhai led to the flourishing of Islamic scholarship and the creation of renowned centers of learning like Timbuktu.

**175. The widespread expansion of Christianity in West Africa is most closely associated with the period of...**
A. the Trans-Saharan trade.
B. the rise of the Oyo Empire.
C. European colonialism in the 19th and 20th centuries.
D. the Fulani Jihads.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** While there were early contacts, the major and sustained spread of Christianity throughout West Africa was directly linked to the activities of European missionary societies during the colonial era.

**176. African Independent Churches (AICs) are an example of...**
A. a complete rejection of Christianity.
B. religious syncretism, blending Christian beliefs with African cultural expressions.
C. churches established and controlled by European missionaries.
D. a return to purely indigenous religious practices.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** AICs often broke away from mission-led churches to create a form of Christianity that incorporated African music, dance, leadership styles, and worldviews, demonstrating a process of cultural blending or syncretism.

**177. The intricately woven, symbolic cloth of the Asante and Ewe peoples of Ghana is known as...**
A. Adire.
B. Bogolanfini (Mud cloth).
C. Kente.
D. Akwete.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Kente cloth is a world-renowned textile from Ghana, known for its vibrant colors and complex patterns, each of which has a specific name and symbolic meaning.

**178. Masks in many traditional African societies are primarily used...**
A. as simple wall decorations.
B. as part of a performance or masquerade in rituals and ceremonies.
C. as protective gear in warfare.
D. for trade with Europeans.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Masks are not static art objects but are integral components of dynamic masquerade performances that embody spirits or ancestors during important community rituals.

**179. A common characteristic of traditional African music is the use of...**
A. simple, single rhythms.
B. written musical scores.
C. polyrhythms and call-and-response patterns.
D. only stringed instruments.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The simultaneous playing of multiple complex rhythms (polyrhythm) and the interplay between a soloist and a chorus (call-and-response) are hallmarks of many African musical traditions.

**180. The long narrative poem celebrating the heroic deeds of the founder of the Mali Empire is the...**
A. Epic of Gilgamesh.
B. Epic of Sundiata.
C. Bayajidda Legend.
D. Odyssey.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The Epic of Sundiata (or Sunjata) is a famous Mande oral epic that recounts the life and achievements of Sundiata Keita, the founder of the Mali Empire.

**181. A matrilineal society is one in which...**
A. women hold all political power.
B. descent and inheritance are traced through the mother's line.
C. men are not allowed to marry.
D. only women can be chiefs.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** In a matrilineal system, a person's lineage, family ties, and often their inheritance of property or titles are determined by their relationship with their mother's side of the family.

**182. Proverbs are a form of oral literature that function to...**
A. list the names of past kings.
B. transmit concise expressions of wisdom and cultural values.
C. tell long, detailed stories about animals.
D. provide directions for long-distance trade.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Proverbs are short, pithy sayings that encapsulate the collective wisdom, morals, and philosophy of a community.

**183. The transfer of wealth from the groom's family to the bride's family in marriage is known as...**
A. a dowry.
B. an inheritance.
C. bride-price or bride-wealth.
D. alimony.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Bride-wealth is a common practice in many African societies that serves to formalize the marriage, create an alliance between two families, and recognize the value of the bride and her labor.

**184. The Akan people of Ghana are a well-known example of a society with a...**
A. patrilineal descent system.
B. bilateral descent system.
C. matrilineal descent system.
D. non-existent kinship system.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Akan are one of the most prominent examples of a matrilineal society in West Africa, where lineage and inheritance traditionally pass through the female line.

**185. A key function of an age set was...**
A. to compete against other age sets for political power.
B. to provide a unit for communal labor and military organization.
C. to elect the chief of the community.
D. to preserve written historical records.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Age sets formed a cohesive group that could be mobilized for important community tasks, such as clearing land, building, or forming a warrior contingent for defense.

**186. The Yoruba term for the lesser divinities who are more directly involved in human affairs is...**
A. Abosom.
B. Mamluks.
C. Orisha.
D. Nganga.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** In the Yoruba religion, the Orishas (e.g., Sango, Ogun, Yemoja) are the pantheon of deities who act as intermediaries between humanity and the supreme being, Olodumare.

**187. In West African history, the term 'Jihad' often refers to...**
A. a pilgrimage to a holy site.
B. a form of oral poetry.
C. an Islamic holy war aimed at reform or expansion.
D. a peaceful missionary movement.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Jihads of the 18th and 19th centuries, like that of Usman dan Fodio, were armed movements led by Islamic reformers to purify the practice of Islam and establish new Islamic states.
