**188. Missionary education was often criticized for having a curriculum that was...**
A. too focused on African history.
B. Eurocentric and denigrating of local cultures.
C. technically advanced and practical.
D. taught only in local African languages.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** A common critique of mission schools was that their curricula were based on European models, taught European history and values, and often presented African culture and traditions as inferior or primitive.

**189. The Yoruba trickster figure who often takes the form of a spider in folktales is...**
A. Sundiata.
B. Bayajidda.
C. Ananse.
D. Ogedengbe.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Ananse (or Anansi) the spider is a famous trickster character in West African and Caribbean folklore, originating with the Akan people of Ghana, known for his cunning and wit. (Note: While originating with Akan, the stories are widespread).

**190. The coexistence of Islam, Christianity, and indigenous religions within a single community or even individual is an example of religious...**
A. conflict.
B. decline.
C. pluralism and syncretism.
D. fundamentalism.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The ability of different religious traditions to coexist and for individuals to blend elements from them is a demonstration of religious pluralism and the process of syncretism.

---

### **Chapter 8 & 12: Social Change, Urbanization & Contemporary Issues (191-225)**

**191. During the colonial period, urban growth was often concentrated in...**
A. ancient inland cities like Timbuktu.
B. new coastal capitals and administrative centers.
C. rural agricultural villages.
D. fortified hilltop settlements.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Colonialism reoriented economic and political life towards the coast. New cities like Lagos, Accra, and Dakar were established as ports and administrative hubs, becoming magnets for migration.

**192. A primary "pull" factor for rural-urban migration in post-colonial West Africa is the...**
A. abundance of fertile land in cities.
B. desire to live in traditional family structures.
C. search for better economic opportunities and modern amenities.
D. lack of social interaction in rural areas.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** People are "pulled" to cities by the prospect of finding jobs, accessing better schools and healthcare, and experiencing a modern lifestyle, even if the reality is often difficult.

**193. A major goal of post-colonial education reforms was to...**
A. limit access to education to a small elite.
B. Africanize curricula to make them more relevant to local history and culture.
C. focus exclusively on religious instruction.
D. adopt the French education system in all countries.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** After independence, a key objective was to move away from Eurocentric colonial curricula and develop educational content that reflected the history, culture, and needs of the new African nations.

**194. The "brain drain" phenomenon refers to the...**
A. decline in educational standards within a country.
B. emigration of highly educated and skilled professionals to other countries.
C. loss of indigenous knowledge systems.
D. impact of malnutrition on cognitive development.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The "brain drain" is a major challenge for many developing countries, where doctors, engineers, academics, and other professionals emigrate in search of better opportunities, representing a loss of human capital.

**195. Colonial policies often altered traditional gender roles by...**
A. promoting women into top administrative positions.
B. favoring men for wage labor and cash crop production, confining many women to the subsistence sector.
C. creating gender equality in all spheres of life.
D. introducing a matrilineal system of inheritance everywhere.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The colonial economy often created a gendered division of labor, drawing men into the formal, cash-based economy while marginalizing or reinforcing women's roles in the non-waged, subsistence economy.

**196. What has been a major transformative technology in Africa in the 21st century?**
A. The telegraph.
B. The landline telephone.
C. The mobile phone.
D. The steam engine.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The rapid and widespread adoption of mobile phones has revolutionized communication, banking (mobile money), business, and social interaction across the continent.

**197. The #EndSARS movement in Nigeria (2020) was a youth-led protest primarily against...**
A. climate change.
B. economic policies.
C. police brutality.
D. electoral fraud.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The #EndSARS protests were sparked by widespread anger over the abuses, extortion, and extrajudicial killings committed by the Special Anti-Robbery Squad (SARS), a notorious police unit.

**198. The #BringBackOurGirls campaign was launched to advocate for the rescue of schoolgirls abducted by...**
A. the Nigerian army.
B. Boko Haram.
C. Niger Delta militants.
D. colonial officials.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The movement started in 2014 to draw global attention to the abduction of over 200 schoolgirls from Chibok, Nigeria, by the Boko Haram terrorist group.

**199. The Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) are a set of global goals adopted by the...**
A. African Union.
B. World Bank.
C. United Nations.
D. European Union.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The SDGs are a universal call to action adopted by all UN Member States in 2015 to address global challenges like poverty, inequality, and climate change by 2030.

**200. Africa is particularly vulnerable to climate change despite...**
A. being the largest contributor to global emissions.
B. having the most advanced adaptation technology.
C. contributing the least to historical global emissions.
D. having a climate that does not change.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** A key injustice of climate change is that Africa, which has contributed negligibly to the greenhouse gas emissions that cause it, is one of the continents most severely affected by its impacts.

**201. The Africa CDC (Centres for Disease Control and Prevention) is an institution of the...**
A. World Health Organization (WHO).
B. African Union (AU).
C. United Nations (UN).
D. ECOWAS.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The Africa CDC is a specialized technical agency of the African Union, established to support member states' public health initiatives and strengthen their capacity to respond to health emergencies.

**202. The term "digital divide" in the African context refers to the...**
A. gap between different social media platforms.
B. disparity in access to the internet and digital technology between different groups.
C. generational conflict over technology use.
D. separation between hardware and software.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The "digital divide" highlights the inequality in access to digital tools like the internet, often seen between urban and rural areas, or between different income and education levels.

**203. A major social impact of widespread school closures during the COVID-19 pandemic in West Africa was...**
A. improved learning outcomes for all students.
B. the challenge of implementing remote learning due to lack of internet and device access.
C. a decrease in educational inequality.
D. a permanent shift to home-schooling for most families.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** While schools closed, the shift to online or remote learning was impossible for the majority of students who lacked the necessary infrastructure (internet, electricity) and devices, thus widening the educational gap.

**204. "Y'en a Marre" in Senegal and "Le Balai Citoyen" in Burkina Faso are examples of...**
A. government-sponsored development programs.
B. youth-led civil society movements advocating for political reform.
C. traditional religious festivals.
D. separatist military groups.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** These are two prominent examples of influential youth movements that used civic action and protest to challenge incumbent leaders and advocate for democracy and good governance.

**205. An "adaptation measure" in response to climate change in Africa would be...**
A. building more coal-fired power plants.
B. developing and planting drought-resistant crops.
C. increasing the number of cars on the road.
D. denying that climate change is happening.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Adaptation refers to adjusting to the actual or expected impacts of climate change. Developing crops that can survive with less water is a key adaptation strategy for ensuring food security in regions facing increased drought.

**206. A "primate city" is a term used in urban studies to describe...**
A. a city that is the oldest in a country.
B. a city located in a jungle.
C. a city that is disproportionately larger and more dominant than any other city in the country.
D. a capital city that is not the largest city.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Many African countries have a primate city (e.g., Lagos, Dakar) that is the overwhelming center of political, economic, and cultural life, dwarfing all other urban centers in the nation.

**207. A Government Reservation Area (GRA) was a feature of colonial cities that served as...**
A. a marketplace for local goods.
B. a segregated, well-serviced residential area for European officials.
C. a recruitment center for the colonial army.
D. a nature reserve for wildlife.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** GRAs were a clear manifestation of racial segregation in colonial urban planning, representing the exclusive, better-resourced neighborhoods where the European colonizers lived.

**208. The expansion of mobile banking services like M-Pesa is an example of the growth of which sector in Africa?**
A. AgriTech.
B. EdTech.
C. HealthTech.
D. Fintech.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** Fintech, or Financial Technology, refers to the use of technology to improve and automate financial services. Mobile money is a prime example of a Fintech innovation that has had a massive impact in Africa.

**209. One of the greatest challenges to the success of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) in Africa is...**
A. a lack of clear targets.
B. the continent's rapid economic growth.
C. ongoing conflict, political instability, and funding constraints.
D. the rejection of the goals by most African leaders.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The text indicates that progress towards the SDGs is mixed and has been severely hampered by persistent challenges such as conflict, weak governance, economic shocks, and a lack of sufficient financial resources.

**210. A key reason many African countries had relatively low official death rates in the early phases of the COVID-19 pandemic was thought to be their...**
A. advanced hospital infrastructure.
B. much younger population demographic.
C. complete isolation from the rest of the world.
D. universal access to vaccines from the start.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** One of the most cited factors for Africa's initially lower-than-expected mortality rates was its demographic structure, with a very high proportion of young people, who were less likely to suffer severe outcomes from the virus.

**211. The issue of "vaccine nationalism" during the COVID-19 pandemic referred to...**
A. African nations refusing to accept any foreign vaccines.
B. wealthy countries hoarding vaccine supplies for their own populations first.
C. the development of unique vaccines for each nation.
D. protests against mandatory vaccination policies.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** "Vaccine nationalism" describes the behavior of high-income countries buying up and reserving large quantities of vaccines, which led to significant delays and shortages for lower-income regions like Africa.

**212. What role does social media play in modern African political movements?**
A. It is used exclusively by governments to spread propaganda.
B. It has no impact on political mobilization.
C. It is a powerful tool for mobilization and information sharing, but also for spreading misinformation.
D. It has completely replaced traditional forms of protest.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Social media is a double-edged sword; it is a vital instrument for activists to organize and raise awareness (as in #EndSARS), but it is also a vehicle for the rapid spread of false information and can be used for state surveillance.

**213. Universal Primary Education (UPE) was a major post-colonial policy aimed at...**
A. restricting education to the wealthy.
B. expanding access to basic education for all children.
C. focusing education solely on university-level studies.
D. privatizing all primary schools.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** UPE was a common and ambitious goal for many newly independent African nations, reflecting the priority placed on expanding educational access to build a skilled citizenry for national development.

**214. A major challenge for the growth of the digital economy in many parts of Africa remains the...**
A. lack of interest from the youth population.
B. government ban on mobile phones.
C. deficit in infrastructure, particularly reliable electricity and internet access.
D. refusal of banks to engage with technology.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** While the digital revolution is underway, its full potential is hampered by fundamental infrastructure challenges, including inconsistent electricity supply and the high cost or lack of widespread, reliable internet.

**215. The African Continental Free Trade Area (AfCFTA) is a major contemporary initiative aimed at...**
A. increasing trade with Europe and Asia.
B. boosting intra-African trade by creating a single market.
C. establishing a single African currency.
D. creating a military alliance against external threats.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The AfCFTA is a flagship project of the AU designed to create one of the world's largest free-trade areas, with the goal of dramatically increasing trade between African countries themselves.

**216. Colonialism impacted African family structures by...**
A. strengthening the extended family system in all areas.
B. promoting a shift towards more nuclear families, especially in urban areas.
C. introducing polyandry as the main form of marriage.
D. eliminating the role of elders in family life.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The forces of urbanization, wage labor, and migration, all spurred by colonialism and its aftermath, contributed to a gradual shift away from the traditional, large extended family unit towards smaller, nuclear family structures.

**217. Women's movements in post-colonial Africa have often campaigned against...**
A. increased access to education for girls.
B. harmful traditional practices like FGM and child marriage.
C. the right of women to participate in politics.
D. the establishment of modern healthcare facilities.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** A key focus of many women's rights organizations has been to challenge and advocate for the elimination of traditional practices that are harmful to the health and rights of women and girls.

**218. The growth of the Nollywood film industry is a powerful example of Africa's...**
A. technological backwardness.
B. inability to compete globally.
C. vibrant and influential creative industries.
D. rejection of all modern media.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Nigeria's Nollywood is a major cultural and economic force, demonstrating the dynamism of African creative industries and their ability to create globally recognized content.

**219. A major success of public health campaigns in post-colonial Africa has been the...**
A. complete eradication of malaria.
B. significant reduction in infant mortality through programs like vaccination.
C. elimination of all traditional medicine.
D. prevention of the COVID-19 pandemic from reaching the continent.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Despite many challenges, concerted public health efforts, particularly routine childhood immunization programs, have led to dramatic and life-saving reductions in infant and child mortality across the continent.

**220. The concept of a "youth bulge" in Africa's demography presents...**
A. only challenges, with no opportunities.
B. only opportunities, with no challenges.
C. both a potential "demographic dividend" and the risk of social unrest if jobs are not created.
D. a situation where the elderly population outnumbers the youth.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** A large youth population can be a huge economic asset (a "demographic dividend") if they are educated and employed. However, if opportunities are scarce, it can also lead to frustration, instability, and social unrest.

**221. An example of a climate change mitigation strategy for an African country would be...**
A. building sea walls to protect against rising sea levels.
B. switching to drought-resistant maize.
C. investing in large-scale solar power projects.
D. creating an early warning system for floods.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Mitigation refers to actions that reduce greenhouse gas emissions. Investing in renewable energy sources like solar power instead of fossil fuels is a key mitigation strategy. (A, B, and D are adaptation strategies).

**222. The term "informal sector" or "informal economy" refers to...**
A. all government-related jobs.
B. economic activities that are not regulated or taxed by the state.
C. large multinational corporations.
D. the agricultural export market.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The informal economy includes a vast range of activities, from street vending and small-scale artisanship to transportation services, which operate outside of formal government regulation and are a primary source of employment in many African cities.

**223. The COVAX facility was a global initiative during the COVID-19 pandemic that aimed to...**
A. develop a single global vaccine.
B. ensure equitable access to vaccines for lower-income countries.
C. fund research into the origins of the virus.
D. enforce global lockdown measures.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** COVAX was co-led by the WHO and others with the goal of pooling resources to procure and distribute vaccines equitably, though it faced significant challenges in achieving this goal.

**224. Which of these is NOT a characteristic of post-colonial urbanization in West Africa?**
A. Rapid and often unplanned growth.
B. Strain on urban infrastructure and services.
C. The growth of large informal settlements.
D. A decrease in the total population of cities.
**Answer: D**
**Explanation:** A defining feature of post-colonial urbanization is the rapid and continuous *increase* in the population of cities, driven by high birth rates and rural-urban migration.

**225. A major reason for creating the African Union (AU) to replace the OAU was the...**
A. need for a weaker organization with less power.
B. desire to re-establish colonial rule.
C. perception that the OAU's principle of non-interference was inadequate for dealing with modern conflicts.
D. success of the OAU in preventing all wars in Africa.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The OAU was often criticized as a "dictators' club" because its strict non-interference policy prevented it from intervening to stop atrocities within member states. The AU was designed with a stronger mandate to address these issues.

---

### **Chapter 13: Approaches to Historical Research (Applied) (226-250)**

**226. In historical research, a bibliography from a scholarly book is useful for...**
A. copying the author's arguments directly.
B. identifying other relevant primary and secondary sources.
C. proving that the book is a primary source.
D. checking the book for spelling errors.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The bibliography or works cited list is a valuable tool for a researcher as it provides a list of the sources the author used, which can lead to other important books, articles, and archival materials on the topic.

**227. When conducting an oral history interview, it is essential to first...**
A. obtain informed consent from the interviewee.
B. write the final report before the interview.
C. ask leading questions to get the desired answers.
D. correct the interviewee's memory lapses as they speak.
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** Ethical practice in oral history demands that the researcher obtains informed consent, ensuring the interviewee understands the project's purpose, how their story will be used, and what their rights are.

**228. The process of converting a recorded interview into a written text is called...**
A. Transcription.
B. Translation.
C. Interpretation.
D. Summarization.
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** Transcription is the specific term for the meticulous process of creating a written version of an audio or video recording.

**229. A "finding aid" in an archive is a tool designed to...**
A. help researchers locate relevant records within a collection.
B. criticize the contents of the documents.
C. provide a published version of all the documents.
D. translate documents into different languages.
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** Finding aids are catalogs, inventories, or guides created by archivists to describe the contents of their collections, helping researchers navigate the materials and find what they need.

**2IBC. When handling original documents in an archive, a researcher should typically...**
A. use a pen to take notes directly on the document.
B. wear clean gloves or have clean hands and use a pencil for notetaking.
C. rearrange the documents into a more logical order.
D. eat and drink to stay energized during research.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Preserving fragile historical documents is paramount. Archives have strict handling rules, which almost always include using only pencils (to avoid permanent marks) and keeping hands clean to prevent damage from oils.

**231. A thesis statement in a historical essay should be...**
A. a question that the essay will answer.
B. a simple statement of fact that everyone agrees with.
C. a clear, specific, and arguable claim that the essay will prove.
D. a list of all the topics that will be mentioned in the essay.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** A strong thesis is not a fact or a question, but an arguable interpretation or argument that presents the author's main point and guides the entire essay.

**232. Using someone else's words or ideas without proper citation is known as...**
A. Historiography.
B. Corroboration.
C. Plagiarism.
D. Paraphrasing.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Plagiarism is a serious academic offense that involves presenting another person's work or ideas as one's own without giving proper credit through citation.

**233. The Chicago Manual of Style is commonly used in history for...**
A. evaluating the authenticity of a source.
B. conducting archaeological dating.
C. formatting citations and bibliographies.
D. analyzing the chemical composition of old paper.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** The Chicago Manual of Style is one of the most widely used citation style guides for historical writing, particularly its notes-bibliography system.

**234. When evaluating a primary source, considering the author's purpose, potential bias, and intended audience is part of...**
A. External Criticism.
B. Chronology.
C. Internal Criticism.
D. Transcription.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Internal Criticism moves beyond authenticity to assess the reliability and credibility of the content within the source, which requires an analysis of the author's perspective and motivations.

**235. An open-ended question is most effective in an oral history interview because it...**
A. can be answered with a simple "yes" or "no".
B. limits the interviewee to a narrow range of topics.
C. encourages the interviewee to provide detailed, narrative answers.
D. confuses the interviewee.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Open-ended questions (e.g., "Tell me about...") allow the narrator to control the direction of their story and provide rich, detailed recollections, rather than being confined by the interviewer's assumptions.

**236. A historian comparing information from multiple sources to check for consistency is practicing...**
A. Corroboration.
B. Fabrication.
C. Internal Criticism.
D. Transcription.
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** Corroboration is the critical method of cross-referencing multiple sources to confirm details, identify discrepancies, and build a more reliable account of an event.

**237. Which of the following would be found in a national archive?**
A. A student's history essay.
B. A recently published history textbook.
C. Official government records, such as census data and legislative reports.
D. A fictional novel set in a historical period.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** National archives are the official repositories for the records created by a country's government in the course of its business.

**238. What is the primary purpose of a conclusion in a historical essay?**
A. To introduce new evidence and arguments.
B. To restate the thesis and summarize the main points of the argument.
C. To ask a new set of questions for the reader to consider.
D. To provide a detailed narrative of events.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** A strong conclusion should not introduce new information but should synthesize the arguments made in the essay, reinforcing the thesis and providing a sense of closure.

**239. The main difference between oral history and oral tradition is that oral history...**
A. is always written down.
B. is passed down through many generations.
C. collects an individual's personal memories from their own lifetime.
D. is less reliable than oral tradition.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Oral history is a research method focused on collecting firsthand recollections from people about their own experiences, whereas oral tradition refers to stories and knowledge passed down from one generation to the next.

**240. The provenance of a document refers to its...**
A. history of ownership, origin, and custody.
B. main argument or thesis.
C. physical condition.
D. written content.
**Answer: A**
**Explanation:** Provenance is a key concept in archival science and source criticism that traces a document's history, which helps to establish its authenticity and context.

**241. A historian who analyzes a photograph by considering who took it and why is evaluating its...**
A. material composition.
B. aesthetic quality.
C. perspective and potential bias.
D. chronological placement.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Just like written sources, visual sources are not objective windows into the past. They are created by people with specific viewpoints and purposes, which must be critically analyzed.

**242. The body paragraphs of a historical essay should each contain...**
A. a thesis statement.
B. a topic sentence, evidence, and analysis.
C. a summary of the entire essay.
D. a list of sources.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The standard structure for a body paragraph is to begin with a topic sentence that makes a point supporting the thesis, followed by evidence from sources, and then analysis that explains how the evidence proves the point.

**243. The first step in any historical research project is to...**
A. write the conclusion.
B. formulate a clear research question.
C. create a detailed bibliography.
D. visit an archive.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** A focused research question is essential because it guides the entire research process, helping the historian to identify relevant sources and maintain a clear argument.

**244. Digital repositories have made archival research easier by...**
A. eliminating the need for source criticism.
B. providing online access to digitized historical documents.
C. guaranteeing that every historical document is authentic.
D. making all archives free to visit.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** The main advantage of digital repositories and online archives is that they allow researchers to access vast collections of materials remotely, without having to travel to the physical location.

**245. "Active listening" is a crucial technique in oral history that involves...**
A. interrupting the narrator frequently to ask questions.
B. focusing on taking notes rather than looking at the narrator.
C. paying close attention and showing interest to build rapport and encourage the narrator.
D. thinking about the next question while the narrator is speaking.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Active listening—showing empathy, making eye contact, and using non-verbal cues—is vital for making the narrator feel comfortable and respected, which leads to a more open and fruitful interview.

**246. What is the primary weakness of relying solely on secondary sources for historical research?**
A. They are usually too old to be useful.
B. They are always biased and inaccurate.
C. The researcher is relying on another historian's interpretation rather than analyzing the raw evidence directly.
D. They are more difficult to find than primary sources.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** While essential for context, relying only on secondary sources means the research is just a summary of other people's arguments. Original historical research requires direct engagement with and interpretation of primary sources.

**247. A footnote or endnote is used in a historical essay primarily to...**
A. provide definitions of difficult words.
B. provide a citation for a source of information or a quotation.
C. add the author's personal opinions.
D. list the essay's main arguments.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Footnotes and endnotes are the primary method in many history citation styles for attributing information to its source, allowing for academic honesty and verification.

**248. Contextualization in historical inquiry means...**
A. arranging events in chronological order.
B. judging past events by modern standards.
C. placing an event or source within its broader historical and cultural setting.
D. memorizing the context of a textbook.
**Answer: C**
**Explanation:** Contextualization is the critical skill of understanding an event, action, or idea by situating it within the specific circumstances, values, and knowledge of its time period.

**249. Which of the following is an ethical consideration in oral history?**
A. Publishing the interview as quickly as possible.
B. Ensuring the anonymity of the interviewee if they request it.
C. Paying the interviewee for a more favorable story.
D. Editing the transcript to change the meaning of the narrator's story.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** Protecting the narrator is a key ethical responsibility. This includes respecting their wishes for confidentiality or anonymity, especially when dealing with sensitive topics.

**250. A key difference between a historical narrative and a simple chronicle is that a narrative...**
A. only lists dates and events in order.
B. seeks to construct an explanation of past events, analyzing causes and effects.
C. is always a primary source.
D. is a fictional account of the past.
**Answer: B**
**Explanation:** A chronicle is a mere record of events in order. A historical narrative, as constructed by a historian, is an interpretation that weaves those events into a coherent story with an argument about why things happened as they did.

Chapter 1: Introduction to History and Historiography

1. According to the text, the Greek word 'historia', from which 'history' is derived, means
A. a collection of dates and names.
B. inquiry, knowledge acquired by investigation.
C. the study of great leaders and wars.
D. a record of what happened in the past.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text explicitly states that History comes from the Greek word 'historia', meaning "inquiry, knowledge acquired by investigation," highlighting its nature as an investigative discipline.

2. Which of the following is NOT listed as a reason for studying history?
A. Developing critical thinking skills
B. Cultivating empathy and perspective
C. Guaranteeing future economic success
D. Understanding the present world
Answer: C
Explanation: The text lists understanding the present, learning from the past, critical thinking, empathy, identity, and informed citizenship as reasons to study history. It does not suggest it guarantees future economic success.

3. A historian studying the development of democratic institutions and the history of political parties is most likely specializing in
A. Social History.
B. Economic History.
C. Cultural History.
D. Political History.
Answer: D
Explanation: The text defines Political History as focusing on political events, leaders, governments, and the development of political institutions and parties.

4. The branch of history that focuses on the everyday lives, social structures, and experiences of ordinary people "from the bottom up" is called
A. Political History.
B. Social History.
C. Diplomatic History.
D. Military History.
Answer: B
Explanation: Social History is defined as exploring the lives and experiences of ordinary people, their family life, gender roles, and class structures, often described as a "from the bottom up" approach.

5. A study of the Industrial Revolution and its impact on labor movements would primarily fall under which branch of history?
A. Cultural History
B. Social History
C. Economic History
D. Intellectual History
Answer: C
Explanation: Economic History focuses on economic systems, production, labor, and the causes and consequences of major economic changes like the Industrial Revolution.

6. The study of beliefs, values, ideas, and symbolic systems of past societies is the primary focus of
A. Political History.
B. Cultural History.
C. Economic History.
D. Social History.
Answer: B
Explanation: Cultural History is described as focusing on beliefs, values, arts, traditions, and how people made sense of their world.

7. A letter written by a soldier during a war is an example of a
A. secondary source.
B. primary source.
C. historiographical text.
D. tertiary source.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text defines a primary source as an original, firsthand account or document created by a witness or participant during the historical period being studied, such as a soldier's letter.

8. Which of the following is the best example of a secondary source?
A. A diary entry from a 19th-century trader
B. A history textbook chapter about the slave trade
C. A photograph of a colonial-era building
D. Government census data from 1960
Answer: B
Explanation: A history textbook, like the one provided, is a secondary source because it is an account created by someone who was not a direct witness, analyzing and synthesizing primary sources.

9. A major advantage of written records as historical sources is that they
A. are always objective and unbiased.
B. can provide detailed and relatively stable information over time.
C. represent the views of all classes of society equally.
D. are indestructible and easily preserved.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text states that a key advantage of written records is their ability to provide detailed, precise, and relatively stable information if they are preserved.

10. A significant limitation of oral traditions as historical sources is the
A. complete lack of historical information.
B. inability to preserve non-elite perspectives.
C. potential for distortion during transmission over time.
D. focus on economic data over cultural values.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text lists potential for distortion, embellishment, or memory lapses during transmission as a key limitation of oral traditions.

11. Checking a document for anachronisms to determine if it is genuine or a forgery is an aspect of
A. Internal Criticism.
B. Contextualization.
C. External Criticism.
D. Historiography.
Answer: C
Explanation: External Criticism is the process of determining the authenticity of a source, which includes checking for anachronisms (things out of place for the period).

12. When a historian evaluates the reliability of a source by considering the author's bias and purpose, they are engaging in
A. External Criticism.
B. Internal Criticism.
C. Chronology.
D. Oral History.
Answer: B
Explanation: Internal Criticism involves assessing the credibility and trustworthiness of the information within an authentic source, including considering the author's background, bias, and purpose.

13. The study of how history itself is written and interpreted is known as
A. Archaeology.
B. Historiography.
C. Chronology.
D. Contextualization.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text defines historiography as "the study of the writing of history; the history of historical thought and interpretation."

14. A school of historiography that emphasizes class struggle and economic factors as the primary drivers of historical change is
A. Feminist Historiography.
B. Nationalist Historiography.
C. Revisionist Historiography.
D. Marxist Historiography.
Answer: D
Explanation: Marxist Historiography, based on the theories of Karl Marx, is described as emphasizing economic factors and class struggle as the main forces in history.

15. The historiographical approach that aims to re-center historical narratives from the perspective of African peoples and challenge Eurocentric biases is called
A. Afrocentric Historiography.
B. Revisionist Historiography.
C. Whig History.
D. Annales School.
Answer: A
Explanation: Afrocentric Historiography is defined as aiming to re-center history from the perspective of African peoples, challenging Eurocentric views and emphasizing African agency.

Chapter 2: Early Civilizations in Africa

A. bronze sculptures using the lost-wax method.

Explanation: The Nok civilization, located in central Nigeria, is best known for its unique and ancient terracotta sculptures of human heads and figures.

17. A key technological advancement of the Nok civilization was
A. the development of papyrus.

A. Nok civilization.
B. Ife civilization.

Explanation: The Ife civilization is renowned for its highly naturalistic and sophisticated sculptures in terracotta and bronze, often representing royalty.

21. The Benin Kingdom was particularly famous for its magnificent bronze plaques and commemorative heads of its rulers, known as
A. Onis.
B. Mais.
C. Sarkis.
D. Obas.

22. Many of the famous Benin bronzes were looted by the
A. Portuguese in the 15th century.

C. British during an expedition in 1897.

23. The rulers of ancient Egypt, who were considered divine intermediaries, were called
A. Viziers.
B. Nomarchs.
C. Pharaohs.
D. Scribes.
Answer: C
Explanation: The pharaohs were the rulers of ancient Egypt, considered divine or semi-divine kings with absolute political and religious authority.

24. The Great Pyramids of Giza were primarily built during the
A. New Kingdom.
B. Middle Kingdom.
C. Old Kingdom.
D. Late Period.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text identifies the Old Kingdom as the "Age of Pyramids," when massive stone monuments like those at Giza were built as tombs for pharaohs.

25. The fundamental concept of cosmic order, justice, and harmony in ancient Egyptian belief was known as
A. Ma'at.
B. Ra.
C. Osiris.
D. Anubis.
Answer: A
Explanation: The text states that the concept of Ma'at (order, truth, justice) was fundamental to Egyptian religious and political life, and the pharaoh was responsible for maintaining it.

26. In the ancient Egyptian social hierarchy, the group that formed the majority of the population was the
A. Scribes.
B. Soldiers.
C. Artisans.
D. Farmers.
Answer: D
Explanation: The text shows a social hierarchy where farmers constituted the majority of the population, below artisans, merchants, and officials.

27. The most important animal for transport in the Trans-Saharan trade was the
A. Horse.
B. Donkey.
C. Camel.
D. Ox.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text highlights that camels, known as "ships of the desert," were the primary means of transport across the Sahara due to their adaptation to desert conditions.

28. What were the two most important commodities exchanged in the Trans-Saharan trade?
A. Gold and Salt
B. Ivory and Horses
C. Slaves and Textiles
D. Kola nuts and Dates
Answer: A
Explanation: The text emphasizes that gold from West Africa and salt from the Sahara were the key commodities, often traded at near-equal value.

29. A major long-term impact of the Trans-Saharan trade on West Africa was the
A. introduction of Christianity.
B. decline of major urban centers.
C. spread of Islam.
D. prevention of state formation.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text clearly states that Arab and Berber traders brought Islam to West Africa along the trade routes, leading to its adoption by rulers and the growth of Islamic centers of learning.

30. The wealth derived from controlling the Trans-Saharan trade routes contributed directly to the rise of the
A. Benin Kingdom.
B. Ife Civilization.
C. Sudanic empires like Ghana, Mali, and Songhai.
D. Nok Civilization.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text states that the trade stimulated the growth of powerful West African empires like Ghana, Mali, and Songhai, which controlled the gold sources and trade routes.

31. The traditional ruler of a Hausa city-state was known as the
A. Oba.
B. Mai.
C. Sarki.
D. Alaafin.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text specifies that the Hausa city-states were each ruled by a king called a Sarki.

32. The Sokoto Caliphate was established in the early 19th century as a result of a jihad led by
A. Ahmadu Bello.
B. Samori Ture.
C. Usman dan Fodio.
D. The Mai of Bornu.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text describes the Fulani Jihad led by Usman dan Fodio, an Islamic scholar, which overthrew Hausa rulers and established the Sokoto Caliphate.

33. The long-lasting empire centered around Lake Chad, ruled by the Sefuwa dynasty, was the
A. Oyo Empire.
B. Songhai Empire.
C. Kanem-Bornu Empire.
D. Asante Kingdom.
Answer: C
Explanation: The Kanem-Bornu Empire was located around Lake Chad and was one of the longest-lasting empires in Africa, ruled by the Mai of the Sefuwa dynasty.

34. In the Oyo Empire, the council of seven principal chiefs that served as a check on the Alaafin's power was the
A. Ogboni Society.
B. Oyo Mesi.
C. Sefuwa Dynasty.
D. Banza Bakwai.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text describes the Oyo Mesi as the council of seven chiefs, led by the Bashorun, who had the power to select and even depose the Alaafin.

35. The military strength of the Oyo Empire, which allowed for its expansion, was particularly renowned for its
A. use of firearms.
B. naval fleet.
C. cavalry.
D. longbow archers.
Answer: C
Explanation: The Oyo Empire's military was especially famous for its strong cavalry, which was crucial for its territorial expansion.

Chapter 3: The Slave Trades

36. The system of trade involving the transport of manufactured goods from Europe to Africa, enslaved Africans to the Americas, and plantation crops back to Europe is known as the
A. Trans-Saharan Trade.
B. Columbian Exchange.
C. Triangular Trade.
D. Mercantilist System.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text describes the Triangular Trade as a three-legged model: goods from Europe to Africa, enslaved people from Africa to the Americas, and crops from the Americas to Europe.

37. The brutal sea voyage that transported enslaved Africans from Africa to the Americas was called the
A. Second Leg.
B. Middle Passage.
C. Atlantic Crossing.
D. Final Voyage.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text identifies the Middle Passage as the second leg of the Triangular Trade, the horrific sea voyage from Africa to the Americas characterized by inhumane conditions and high mortality.

38. The primary driver for the massive scale of the Transatlantic Slave Trade was the
A. European desire for African soldiers.
B. demand for labor on plantations in the Americas.
C. need to depopulate West Africa.
D. pre-existing African desire to sell people.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text clearly states that the demand for labor on large-scale plantations growing cash crops like sugar and cotton in the Americas was the main driver of the trade.

39. How did Europeans most commonly acquire enslaved people in Africa for the Transatlantic trade?
A. By directly raiding deep into the African interior themselves
B. Through trading manufactured goods like guns with African rulers and merchants on the coast
C. By capturing only criminals and debtors
D. By setting up their own plantations in Africa
Answer: B
Explanation: While initial raids occurred, the text explains that the more common method was for Europeans to establish coastal forts and obtain slaves from African intermediaries in exchange for goods.

40. A major economic impact of the slave trades on West Africa was
A. rapid industrialization.
B. a significant loss of productive population.
C. the development of a balanced internal economy.
D. a decrease in warfare and instability.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text lists depopulation, the loss of millions of young, productive individuals, as a major negative economic impact on West Africa.

41. The demand for slaves in the Transatlantic trade led to which major social impact within West Africa?
A. Increased inter-ethnic unity and trust
B. The empowerment of women in political roles
C. A decrease in social stratification
D. An increase in warfare, raiding, and general insecurity
Answer: D
Explanation: The text states that the demand for slaves fueled inter-state wars and raids as communities sought captives for trade, leading to widespread instability.

42. The philosophical and intellectual movement in 18th-century Europe that emphasized human rights and questioned the morality of slavery was the
A. Reformation.
B. Renaissance.
C. Enlightenment.
D. Industrial Revolution.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text cites Enlightenment ideas from philosophers like Montesquieu and Rousseau as a key factor in the rise of abolitionism.

43. Which religious group was among the earliest and most consistent opponents of slavery?
A. Anglicans
B. Catholics
C. Quakers
D. Lutherans
Answer: C
Explanation: The text identifies the Quakers (Society of Friends) as being among the earliest and most consistent opponents of slavery on moral and religious grounds.

44. The successful slave revolt that led to the first independent black republic and was a major blow to the institution of slavery took place in
A. Jamaica.
B. Brazil.
C. Haiti (Saint-Domingue).
D. Cuba.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text highlights the Haitian Revolution (1791-1804) as a major act of slave resistance that resulted in the independent republic of Haiti and inspired abolitionists.

45. In what year did Great Britain abolish the slave trade throughout its empire?
A. 1776
B. 1807
C. 1833
D. 1865
Answer: B
Explanation: The text specifies that Great Britain passed legislation to abolish the slave trade in 1807. The abolition of the institution of slavery itself came later in 1833.

46. The abolition of slavery in the United States was formally achieved with the
A. Emancipation Proclamation.
B. end of the Civil War.
C. 13th Amendment to the Constitution.
D. Missouri Compromise.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text states that the 13th Amendment to the Constitution in 1865 abolished slavery in the United States.

47. The worldwide dispersal of people of African descent, primarily due to the slave trade, is known as the
A. Great Migration.
B. Scramble for Africa.
C. African Diaspora.
D. Columbian Exchange.
Answer: C
Explanation: The African Diaspora is defined as the dispersal of millions of people of African descent throughout the Americas and other parts of the world as a result of the slave trade.

48. Jazz, blues, reggae, and samba are examples of musical genres that
A. were brought from Europe to Africa.
B. originated in Africa and remained unchanged.
C. have deep roots in the cultural contributions of the African diaspora.
D. were developed to support the institution of slavery.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text lists these musical forms as examples of rich cultural contributions made by enslaved Africans and their descendants in the Americas.

49. The development of racist ideologies was used to
A. encourage the abolition of slavery.
B. justify the enslavement of Africans.
C. promote unity between Europeans and Africans.
D. understand the economic benefits of free labor.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text clearly states that racist ideologies were developed and reinforced to dehumanize Africans and justify their enslavement.

50. Vodou in Haiti and Santería in Cuba are examples of
A. traditional African religions that remained unchanged.
B. syncretic religions blending African beliefs with Christianity.
C. European religions forced upon enslaved people.
D. political movements that fought for abolition.
Answer: B
Explanation: These are listed as examples of syncretic religions that combined African spiritual beliefs with Christianity, a key cultural legacy of the diaspora.

Chapter 4: European Colonization of Africa

51. The intense period of competition among European powers to annex territory in Africa from c. 1880 to 1914 is known as the
A. Berlin Conference.
B. Scramble for Africa.
C. Age of Discovery.
D. Colonial Partition.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text defines the "Scramble for Africa" as this specific period of intense competition and colonization by European powers.

52. Which of the following was a primary economic motive for the European colonization of Africa?
A. To spread African industrial techniques to Europe
B. To find new homes for Europe's excess population
C. To search for raw materials for European industries
D. To learn about African systems of government
Answer: C
Explanation: A key economic motive listed is the search for raw materials like rubber, minerals, and palm oil to fuel the Industrial Revolution in Europe.

53. The ideological justification for colonialism, which claimed a duty to spread Western civilization, was known as the
A. "White Man's Burden".
B. "Manifest Destiny".
C. "Effective Occupation".
D. "Three Cs".
Answer: A
Explanation: The text identifies the "White Man's Burden," along with Social Darwinism, as an ideological motive used to justify colonialism as a "civilizing mission."

54. The Berlin Conference of 1884-85 was convened to
A. grant independence to African nations.
B. regulate European colonization and avoid conflict among European powers.
C. listen to the demands of African leaders.
D. abolish the slave trade in Africa.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text states the conference was convened by Bismarck to regulate European colonization and trade and to prevent conflicts between the colonizing powers.

55. A significant outcome of the Berlin Conference was the principle of
A. Self-Determination.
B. Effective Occupation.
C. Indirect Rule.
D. Free Trade.
Answer: B
Explanation: The principle of "Effective Occupation" was a key outcome, requiring a power to demonstrate administrative presence to claim a territory, which accelerated the scramble.

56. A major long-term consequence of the way colonial boundaries were drawn in Africa was
A. the perfect alignment of borders with ethnic groups.
B. the grouping of rival ethnic groups into a single colony.
C. the promotion of pan-African unity.
D. economic prosperity for all regions.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text highlights that arbitrary boundaries often grouped historically rival ethnic groups together or divided existing ones, leading to many post-colonial conflicts.

57. The colonial administrative policy favored by the British, which governed through existing traditional rulers, was known as
A. Assimilation.
B. Direct Rule.
C. Indirect Rule.
D. Association.
Answer: C
Explanation: Indirect Rule, championed by Lord Lugard and used by the British, is defined as governing through traditional rulers and institutions under colonial supervision.

58. The French colonial policy that aimed to absorb colonial subjects into French culture and language was called
A. Indirect Rule.
B. Paternalism.
C. Association.
D. Assimilation.
Answer: D
Explanation: The text describes the French policy of "assimilation" as an aim of their Direct Rule system, encouraging subjects to adopt French culture.

59. A primary purpose of building railways and roads in colonial Africa was to
A. promote social integration between African communities.
B. facilitate the extraction of resources and movement of troops.
C. provide jobs for the unemployed in Europe.
D. encourage tourism and cultural exchange.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text explains that infrastructure was built primarily to serve colonial interests, such as extracting raw materials and moving troops, not for integrated national development.

60. The colonial economic policy of encouraging the production of crops for export, often at the expense of food crops, is known as a
A. subsistence economy.
B. diversified economy.
C. cash crop economy.
D. socialist economy.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text states that colonial policies often forced or encouraged the production of cash crops (like cocoa, cotton) for export, which could lead to food insecurity.

61. The Mandinka leader who led a prolonged military resistance against French expansion in West Africa was
A. Menelik II.
B. Yaa Asantewaa.
C. Samori Ture.
D. Shaka Zulu.
Answer: C
Explanation: Samori Ture is cited as a key figure of primary resistance who led the Mandinka Empire in a skillful military campaign against the French for over 15 years.

62. The War of the Golden Stool in 1900 was an Asante uprising led by
A. Samori Ture.
B. Menelik II.
C. The Mahdi.
D. Yaa Asantewaa.
Answer: D
Explanation: Yaa Asantewaa, the Queen Mother of Ejisu, is identified as the leader of the Asante uprising against the British attempt to seize the sacred Golden Stool.

63. Which African nation successfully resisted European colonization by defeating the Italians at the Battle of Adwa in 1896?
A. Egypt
B. Ethiopia
C. Liberia
D. Sudan
Answer: B
Explanation: The text highlights Ethiopia, under Emperor Menelik II, as an exception to the Scramble for Africa, having successfully defeated the Italians at Adwa.

64. Which of the following is considered a negative effect of colonialism in Africa?
A. Introduction of Western education for all
B. Creation of dependent economies
C. Complete eradication of all diseases
D. Peaceful transfer of power
Answer: B
Explanation: Among the negative effects listed, the creation of dependent economies geared towards exporting raw materials is a major one. Education was limited, not for all.

65. A positive, though often unintended, effect of colonialism was the
A. preservation of all indigenous cultures.
B. establishment of a small, educated elite who would later lead nationalist movements.
C. equal distribution of wealth.
D. development of indigenous manufacturing industries.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text notes that while colonial education was limited, it ironically created an educated elite that used Western concepts of democracy to challenge colonial rule.

Chapter 5: Struggle for Independence in West Africa

66. Which of the following was a major factor contributing to the rise of nationalism in West Africa after World War II?
A. The economic boom created by colonial rule
B. The experience of African soldiers who fought in the war
C. The success of the Berlin Conference
D. The universal application of Indirect Rule
Answer: B
Explanation: The experience of African soldiers in WWII, who saw European vulnerability and were exposed to ideas of self-determination, is listed as a key factor in fueling nationalism.

67. The intellectual movement advocating for the unity and self-determination of all people of African descent, which influenced many nationalist leaders, was
A. Pan-Africanism.
B. Socialism.
C. Negritude.
D. Nationalism.
Answer: A
Explanation: Pan-Africanism is defined as the movement advocating for solidarity and self-determination for all people of African descent, which heavily influenced leaders like Nkrumah.

68. The first nationalist party formed in the Gold Coast (Ghana), which advocated for "self-government within the shortest possible time," was the
A. Convention People's Party (CPP).
B. National Council of Nigeria and the Cameroons (NCNC).
C. United Gold Coast Convention (UGCC).
D. Action Group (AG).
Answer: C
Explanation: The UGCC, formed in 1947 by figures like J.B. Danquah, was the first major nationalist party in the Gold Coast.

69. Kwame Nkrumah broke away from the UGCC to form which more radical, mass-based party that demanded "Self-Government Now!"?
A. The Convention People's Party (CPP)
B. The Northern People's Congress (NPC)
C. The Action Group (AG)
D. The National Council of Nigeria and the Cameroons (NCNC)
Answer: A
Explanation: Nkrumah formed the CPP in 1949 as a more radical party with a mass following, using the slogan "Self-Government Now!"

70. The nationalist party in Nigeria led by Nnamdi Azikiwe, which was initially pan-Nigerian but later became dominant in the East, was the
A. Action Group (AG).
B. Northern People's Congress (NPC).
C. National Council of Nigeria and the Cameroons (NCNC).
D. Nigerian National Democratic Party (NNDP).
Answer: C
Explanation: The NCNC was founded by Herbert Macaulay and later led by Nnamdi Azikiwe, becoming a major nationalist force, particularly in Eastern Nigeria.

71. The Action Group (AG), a major political party in Western Nigeria, was led by
A. Nnamdi Azikiwe.
B. Ahmadu Bello.
C. Herbert Macaulay.
D. Obafemi Awolowo.
Answer: D
Explanation: The text identifies Obafemi Awolowo as the leader of the Action Group, a party primarily based among the Yoruba in Western Nigeria.

72. The dominant political party in Northern Nigeria, led by Ahmadu Bello, was the
A. National Council of Nigeria and the Cameroons (NCNC).
B. Action Group (AG).
C. Northern People's Congress (NPC).
D. Convention People's Party (CPP).
Answer: C
Explanation: The NPC, led by Ahmadu Bello, was the primary political party representing the interests of the Hausa-Fulani-dominated Northern Region.

73. Kwame Nkrumah's strategy of non-violent strikes, boycotts, and civil disobedience to achieve independence was called
A. Passive Resistance.
B. Positive Action.
C. Civil Disobedience.
D. Non-Cooperation.
Answer: B
Explanation: Nkrumah's strategy, which he used to put pressure on the British, was explicitly named "Positive Action."

74. The first sub-Saharan African country to gain independence from colonial rule was
A. Nigeria in 1960.
B. Ghana in 1957.
C. Sierra Leone in 1961.
D. Guinea in 1958.
Answer: B
Explanation: Ghana's independence on March 6, 1957, is noted as a landmark event, being the first in sub-Saharan Africa.

75. Nigeria achieved independence on
A. March 6, 1957.
B. October 1, 1960.
C. April 27, 1961.
D. October 2, 1958.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text gives the date of Nigeria's independence as October 1, 1960.

76. Who became the first Prime Minister of an independent Nigeria in 1960?
A. Nnamdi Azikiwe
B. Obafemi Awolowo
C. Abubakar Tafawa Balewa
D. Ahmadu Bello
Answer: C
Explanation: Following the 1959 elections, Abubakar Tafawa Balewa of the NPC became Nigeria's first Prime Minister in a coalition government.

77. The West African nation that gained immediate independence in 1958 by voting "No" to remaining in the French Community was
A. Senegal.
B. Ivory Coast.
C. Guinea.
D. Togo.
Answer: C
Explanation: Guinea, under the leadership of Sékou Touré, uniquely chose immediate independence by rejecting de Gaulle's proposal, leading to an abrupt French withdrawal.

78. One of the most significant political challenges faced by newly independent African nations was
A. a surplus of experienced administrators.
B. forging national unity among diverse ethnic groups.
C. an over-industrialized economy.
D. a lack of international recognition.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text lists "forging a sense of national identity and unity" among diverse ethnic groups brought together by artificial colonial boundaries as a major challenge.

79. The term for the indirect economic and political control exerted by former colonial powers over independent nations is
A. Imperialism.
B. Neo-colonialism.
C. Mercantilism.
D. Federalism.
Answer: B
Explanation: Neo-colonialism is defined as the continued economic dependence and indirect political control that new nations experienced after formal independence.

80. Sir Milton Margai was the leader who led which country to independence in 1961?
A. Ghana
B. The Gambia
C. Nigeria
D. Sierra Leone
Answer: D
Explanation: Sir Milton Margai, leader of the SLPP, is identified as the first Prime Minister who led Sierra Leone to independence in 1961.

Chapter 6: Political Developments Since Independence

81. Kwame Nkrumah's government in Ghana was characterized by a strong promotion of
A. Regionalism.
B. Pan-Africanism.
C. Capitalism.
D. Alignment with the West.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text highlights that Nkrumah was a fervent advocate for Pan-Africanism and African unity, making Accra a hub for nationalists.

82. The 1958 law in Ghana that allowed for detention without trial and was used against political opponents was the
A. Preventive Detention Act.
B. State Security Act.
C. One-Party State Law.
D. Emergency Powers Act.
Answer: A
Explanation: The text explicitly mentions the Preventive Detention Act of 1958 as a key tool used by Nkrumah's government to suppress opposition.

83. In 1964, Ghana under Kwame Nkrumah officially became a
A. federal republic.
B. constitutional monarchy.
C. multi-party democracy.
D. one-party state.
Answer: D
Explanation: The text states that in 1964, Ghana officially became a one-party state under the CPP, with opposition parties suppressed.

84. Kwame Nkrumah was overthrown in a military coup in
A. 1957.
B. 1960.
C. 1966.
D. 1970.
Answer: C
Explanation: Nkrumah's government was ended by a military coup in February 1966 while he was on a state visit abroad.

85. Nigeria's First Republic (1960-1966) was characterized by a
A. unitary system of government.
B. parliamentary system with a federal structure.
C. presidential system with two regions.
D. one-party authoritarian state.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text describes the First Republic as having a parliamentary system based on the British model and a federal structure with three (later four) powerful regions.

86. A major cause of instability during Nigeria's First Republic was
A. the dominance of a single, unified national party.
B. intense ethnic regionalism and rivalry.
C. an economic depression caused by falling oil prices.
D. a lack of constitutional framework.
Answer: B
Explanation: Intense competition for power between the major ethnic and regional blocs (North, West, East) is cited as a key challenge of the First Republic.

87. The first military coup in Nigeria took place in
A. October 1960.
B. January 1966.
C. July 1966.
D. May 1967.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text dates the first military coup, which overthrew the civilian government of the First Republic, to January 15, 1966.

88. The Nigerian Civil War was fought between the Federal Government of Nigeria and the secessionist state of
A. Oduduwa.
B. Biafra.
C. Arewa.
D. Mid-West.
Answer: B
Explanation: The civil war (1967-1970) began after the Eastern Region, under Lt. Col. Ojukwu, seceded and declared the independent Republic of Biafra.

89. The Head of State of Nigeria during the Civil War was
A. Johnson Aguiyi-Ironsi.
B. Chukwuemeka Odumegwu Ojukwu.
C. Yakubu Gowon.
D. Murtala Muhammed.
Answer: C
Explanation: General Yakubu Gowon was the Nigerian Head of State who led the federal side against the Biafran secession.

90. The leader of the Biafran secession was
A. Yakubu Gowon.
B. Nnamdi Azikiwe.
C. Obafemi Awolowo.
D. Chukwuemeka Odumegwu Ojukwu.
Answer: D
Explanation: Lt. Colonel C.O. Ojukwu, military governor of the Eastern Region, declared the region's secession as the Republic of Biafra.

91. At the end of the Nigerian Civil War, General Gowon declared a policy of "No Victor, No Vanquished" and the "3 Rs," which stood for
A. Revolution, Reform, and Rebuilding.
B. Reconciliation, Reabsorption, and Reconstruction.
C. Repatriation, Reparation, and Regionalism.
D. Resistance, Revolt, and Republic.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text states Gowon's post-war policy was one of Reconciliation, Reabsorption, and Reconstruction (the 3 Rs).

92. Nigeria's Second Republic (1979-1983) was overthrown by a military coup led by
A. Olusegun Obasanjo.
B. Ibrahim Babangida.
C. Muhammadu Buhari.
D. Sani Abacha.
Answer: C
Explanation: The civilian government of Shehu Shagari was overthrown in a coup in December 1983, which brought General Muhammadu Buhari to power.

93. The Nigerian military regime that annulled the June 12, 1993 presidential election was led by
A. Sani Abacha.
B. Ibrahim Babangida.
C. Muhammadu Buhari.
D. Abdulsalami Abubakar.
Answer: B
Explanation: The regime of General Ibrahim Babangida is noted for annulling the 1993 election, which was widely believed to have been won by M.K.O. Abiola.

94. The military Head of State who initiated the swift transition that led to Nigeria's Fourth Republic in 1999 was
A. Sani Abacha.
B. Ibrahim Babangida.
C. Abdulsalami Abubakar.
D. Olusegun Obasanjo.
Answer: C
Explanation: After the death of Sani Abacha, General Abdulsalami Abubakar took over and initiated the quick transition program that returned Nigeria to civilian rule in 1999.

95. The primary purpose of the Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS) was initially economic integration, but it has since become heavily involved in
A. promoting cultural festivals.
B. establishing a single currency only.
C. peacekeeping and conflict resolution.
D. regulating colonial boundaries.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text highlights that ECOWAS has evolved to play a significant role in conflict prevention, management, and peacekeeping in the region, for example, through ECOMOG.

96. The peacekeeping force of ECOWAS, deployed to conflict zones like Liberia and Sierra Leone, is known as
A. ECOMOG.
B. UNOWAS.
C. AUPSC.
D. ASF.
Answer: A
Explanation: ECOMOG (ECOWAS Monitoring Group) is the name of the military peacekeeping force deployed by ECOWAS to intervene in regional conflicts.

97. The continental organization that succeeded the Organization of African Unity (OAU) with a stronger mandate for peace and security is the
A. African Union (AU).
B. United Nations (UN).
C. Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS).
D. African Development Bank (AfDB).
Answer: A
Explanation: The African Union (AU) was formed to replace the OAU, with a stronger mandate including the principle of "non-indifference" allowing for intervention in grave circumstances.

98. The AU's principle that allows it to intervene in member states in cases of war crimes or genocide, moving away from the OAU's strict non-interference, is the policy of
A. Non-Alignment.
B. Non-Interference.
C. Non-Indifference.
D. Non-Aggression.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text explicitly contrasts the AU's policy of "Non-Indifference" with the OAU's older "non-interference" principle.

99. The ECOWAS Protocol on Democracy and Good Governance condemns
A. the creation of new political parties.
B. unconstitutional changes of government.
C. federal systems of government.
D. regional economic integration.
Answer: B
Explanation: A key feature of this protocol is that it sets standards for democracy and condemns unconstitutional changes of government, such as military coups.

100. Nigeria's Fourth Republic began in 1999 with the election of which president?
A. Shehu Shagari
B. M.K.O. Abiola
C. Olusegun Obasanjo
D. Goodluck Jonathan
Answer: C
Explanation: The text states that the return to civilian rule in 1999, marking the start of the Fourth Republic, began with Olusegun Obasanjo as president.

Chapter 7: Traditional Societies, Religion, and Culture

101. A kinship system where descent and inheritance are traced through the male line is described as
A. Matrilineal.
B. Bilateral.
C. Patrilineal.
D. Avuncular.
Answer: C
Explanation: Patrilineal descent is defined as tracing lineage and inheritance through the father's side, which the text notes is common in many West African societies.

102. The Akan people of Ghana are mentioned as an example of a society that practices
A. Patrilineal descent.
B. Matrilineal descent.
C. Bilateral descent.
D. Ambilineal descent.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text cites the Akan in Ghana as an example of a group that practices matrilineal descent, tracing lineage through the female line.

103. In many traditional West African societies, social organizations based on age, where individuals pass through stages of life and responsibility collectively, are known as
A. Kinship systems.
B. Clans.
C. Age grades.
D. Chieftaincies.
Answer: C
Explanation: Age grades and age sets are defined as social organizations based on age, with specific roles and duties for each stage of life.

104. Which of the following was NOT typically a role of a traditional ruler in a chieftaincy institution?
A. Political leadership
B. Judicial functions
C. Conducting international diplomacy with Europe
D. Religious and ritual leadership
Answer: C
Explanation: While they had extensive local roles, conducting formal international diplomacy with European powers in the modern sense was not a typical function of traditional rulers before the colonial era.

105. In many traditional political systems, the power of a chief or king was often limited by
A. a written constitution.
B. universal suffrage.
C. councils of elders or secret societies.
D. a neighboring state.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text states that a ruler's power was often not absolute but was checked by institutions like councils of elders, queen mothers, or societies.

106. In many indigenous African religions, the single, all-powerful Creator God is often viewed as
A. directly involved in everyday human affairs.
B. a malevolent and punishing force.
C. remote and distant, with worship directed through intermediaries.
D. one of many equal gods.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text describes the Supreme Being as often being seen as remote, with prayers and rituals directed towards lesser divinities or ancestors as intermediaries.

107. The veneration of deceased family members who are believed to be intermediaries between the living and the spirit world is known as
A. Divination.
B. Ancestor worship.
C. Polytheism.
D. Animism.
Answer: B
Explanation: Ancestor veneration (or worship) is defined as the belief that ancestors exist in the spirit world and can influence the living, acting as intermediaries.

108. The Orishas in Yoruba religion and the Abosom in Akan religion are examples of
A. the Supreme Being.
B. lesser divinities or deities.
C. revered ancestors.
D. evil spirits.
Answer: B
Explanation: These are given as examples of the pantheon of lesser gods, goddesses, or spirits who are more directly involved in human and natural affairs.

109. A practice used to gain insight into the future or diagnose problems by communicating with spirits is called
A. Libation.
B. Ritual.
C. Divination.
D. Sacrifice.
Answer: C
Explanation: Divination is defined as a practice used to gain knowledge of the unknown or the future through specific techniques employed by a diviner.

110. Islam was primarily introduced to West Africa from North Africa through
A. military conquest by the Ottoman Empire.
B. Christian missionaries.
C. Trans-Saharan trade routes.
D. European colonial policies.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text clearly states that Islam was introduced mainly by Arab and Berber traders and scholars along the Trans-Saharan trade routes.

111. The blending of Islamic or Christian beliefs with existing indigenous practices is known as
A. Syncretism.
B. Monotheism.
C. Conversion.
D. Segregation.
Answer: A
Explanation: The text uses the term syncretism to describe the blending of different religious beliefs and practices, which occurred with both Islam and Christianity in Africa.

112. The major spread of Christianity in West Africa occurred primarily in the 19th and 20th centuries and was associated with
A. the Trans-Saharan trade.
B. ancient Roman influence.
C. European colonialism and missionary activities.
D. the jihads of the Sokoto Caliphate.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text links the major expansion of Christianity to the era of European colonialism and the work of Christian missionary societies.

113. The rich tradition of spoken art forms like folktales, proverbs, and epic poetry is referred to as
A. Calligraphy.
B. Scripture.
C. Orature.
D. Hieroglyphics.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text uses the term Orature (Oral Literature) to describe the diverse tradition of spoken art forms that transmit history, values, and knowledge.

114. The intricately woven and symbolic textile of the Asante and Ewe peoples of Ghana is known as
A. Kente cloth.
B. Adire cloth.
C. Mud cloth.
D. Bogolanfini.
Answer: A
Explanation: Kente cloth is specifically mentioned as a famous textile from Ghana, associated with the Asante and Ewe.

115. A long narrative poem celebrating heroic deeds, such as the Epic of Sundiata, is a form of
A. Proverb.
B. Folktale.
C. Epic Poetry.
D. Praise Song.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text defines epic poetry as long narratives about heroes and historical events, citing the Epic of Sundiata as an example.

Chapter 8: Urbanization, Education, and Social Change

116. Cities like Timbuktu, Gao, and Kano are examples of
A. pre-colonial urban centers.
B. cities created by Christian missionaries.
C. colonial administrative capitals.
D. post-colonial industrial hubs.
Answer: A
Explanation: The text identifies these cities as significant urban centers that existed in West Africa before the arrival of European colonizers, often associated with trade and empires.

117. Colonial infrastructure like railways and ports was primarily built to
A. connect different ethnic groups.
B. serve the export-oriented colonial economy.
C. encourage rural development.
D. provide leisure travel for Africans.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text emphasizes that colonial infrastructure was designed to facilitate the extraction of resources and connect them to coastal ports for export, serving colonial economic needs.

118. A common feature of colonial cities was the creation of segregated residential areas, with well-serviced European sections often called
A. African Townships.
B. Government Reservation Areas (GRAs).
C. Central Business Districts (CBDs).
D. Informal Settlements.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text mentions Government Reservation Areas (GRAs) as examples of the planned, better-serviced residential areas for Europeans in colonial cities.

119. The large-scale movement of people from rural areas to cities in search of jobs and modern amenities is known as
A. Rural-urban migration.
B. Forced migration.
C. Urban sprawl.
D. Gentrification.
Answer: A
Explanation: Rural-urban migration is the term used to describe the movement of populations from the countryside to towns and cities, a key driver of post-colonial urbanization.

120. In the 19th and early 20th centuries, Western-style education was first introduced to many parts of West Africa primarily by
A. Colonial governments.
B. Islamic scholars.
C. Christian missionaries.
D. African traditional rulers.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text identifies Christian missionaries as the pioneers in establishing Western-style schools, initially for evangelistic and administrative purposes.

121. The main aim of colonial government education policies was often to
A. provide universal higher education for all Africans.
B. produce high-level scientists and engineers.
C. preserve and promote indigenous cultures.
D. train a small number of low-level clerks and administrators.
Answer: D
Explanation: The text states that colonial education was often limited in scope, aimed at producing the low-level administrative staff needed to run the colonial system.

122. A major goal of post-colonial education reforms was to
A. make curricula more Eurocentric.
B. limit access to higher education.
C. Africanize curricula to be more relevant to local culture and history.
D. focus solely on religious education.
Answer: C
Explanation: "Africanizing curricula" to make them more relevant to the local context and history is listed as a key goal of education reforms after independence.

123. The issue of emigration of educated professionals from African countries to other parts of the world is known as
A. Brain gain.
B. Brain drain.
C. Brain circulation.
D. Brain exchange.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text lists "brain drain" as an ongoing challenge for post-colonial education systems, where educated professionals leave for opportunities elsewhere.

124. In many traditional West African societies, women often held significant economic power through their dominance in
A. long-distance trade.
B. political leadership.
C. local market trade.
D. iron smelting.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text notes that women, particularly market women, often dominated local market trade and held considerable economic power in that sphere.

125. Colonial policies often reinforced patriarchy by
A. giving women equal access to land ownership.
B. favoring men for wage labor and Western education.
C. promoting women to high-level administrative posts.
D. encouraging women's participation in cash crop farming.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text suggests colonial policies often exacerbated gender inequality by favoring men for opportunities like wage labor and education, confining women to the domestic or subsistence sphere.

126. A significant trend in family structures, especially in urban areas, during the post-colonial era is the shift towards
A. more polygynous families.
B. more extended families.
C. more nuclear families.
D. clan-based households.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text identifies a shift from predominantly extended family systems toward more nuclear families (parents and immediate children) as a trend in post-colonial urban Africa.

127. Which of the following was a major social impact of the COVID-19 pandemic in West Africa?
A. A significant increase in rural-urban migration
B. The complete collapse of all health systems
C. Widespread school closures and challenges with remote learning
D. An end to all forms of social media use
Answer: C
Explanation: The text highlights widespread school closures and the difficulty of implementing remote learning due to lack of internet access and devices as a major social impact of the pandemic.

128. An important lesson learned from the COVID-19 and Ebola crises in Africa is the need to
A. rely solely on international aid.
B. strengthen national public health systems and emergency preparedness.
C. close borders permanently.
D. abandon traditional medicine entirely.
Answer: B
Explanation: The text concludes that a key lesson from modern health crises is the importance of strengthening local and national public health infrastructure and preparedness.

129. The term used to describe a single city that overwhelmingly dominates the urban system of a country is a
A. megacity.
B. capital city.
C. primate city.
D. colonial city.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text mentions the development of "large primate cities" as a characteristic of post-colonial urban growth, where one city is disproportionately larger and more influential than others in the country.

130. A major challenge of rapid and unplanned urbanization in post-colonial West Africa is
A. a declining population.
B. a surplus of housing and services.
C. the growth of informal settlements or slums.
D. a lack of cultural dynamism.
Answer: C
Explanation: The text clearly states that the growth of informal settlements (slums) is a significant challenge resulting from rapid urbanization and housing shortages.

Chapter 12: Contemporary African History and the Global 21st Century

131. The increasing interconnectedness of economies, societies, and cultures across the world is known as
A. Nationalism.
B. Regionalism.
C. Globalization.
D. Colonialism.
Answer: C
